APPENDIX 0 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al., 
Plaintiffs, 
vs. 
STATE OF MISSOURI, et al., 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
[Filed July 5, 1991] 
Before the Court is the KCMSD’s motion for approval of the 
desegregation plan for 1991-92. The KCMSD filed an amended 
motion and the State filed a response to the KCMSD’s motion 
and amended motion. The KCMSD filed a reply to the State’s 
response and filed a second motion for leave to amend its motion 
for approval of the desegregation plan for 1991-92. On June 10, 
1991 the Court held a hearing on disputed issues for the 1991-92 
desegregation plan. Parties filed post-hearing briefs on June 21, 
1991. 
The KCMSD moves this Court to enter an order approving its 
proposed desegregation plan for the 1991-92 school year. The 
KCMSD submits that it proposes only a modest one-year exten- 
sion of its present desegregation programs with minimal neces- 
sary modifications. The proposal incorporates 
a number of 
adjustments some of which are based on enrollment driven 
formulas. In addition the KCMSD proposes two new initiatives 
and five magnet school program modifications based on the 
needs of magnet programs. 
The two new initiatives are a 
— A-355 — 
posal 
toi 
ls and to lengthen 
ro 
to improve security 
at secondary schoo 
oa 
school day at four magnet schools. The KCMSD proposes 
to continue the basic educational components of the remedy 
previously approved by the Court. 
The State’s position with regard to the proposed Year Vil 
budget is that the Court should freeze all regular operating and 
desegregation programs at their current levels. The State also 
believes that no new programs should be approved for the 
‘KCMSD in light of the KCMSD’s severe budget crisis. The 
State suggests that if some programs require more funding in 
Year VII, while other programs require less, the increased 
funding for those programs can come from budget transfers from 
those programs which have been reduced from Year VI. The 
State submitted specific objections to the KCMSD’s proposed 
Year VII budget. 
The KCMSD filed a reply to the State’s response. 
The 
KCMSD noted that it considered “freezing” all programs at 
current levels when it developed the Year VII desegregation plan 
and budget. However, the KCMSD submits that the Court 
should 
not adopt the State’s request for 
a freeze because it is not 
consistent with the purpose of the remedy. The KCMSD points 
out that the overall Year VII budget is an increase of approxi- 
mately 3% over the 1990-91 approved budget. 
' 
f the 
The parties have agreed on the following components 0 
Year VII budget with the understandings stated. Components as 
to which there is no dispute are: reduced 
class size, full day 
kindergarten, extended 
day at traditional schools, summer school 
1991, effective schools, base budgets, district communications, 
research and evaluation, interest expense, and the Westport 
computer technician. 
Reduced Class Size 
On July 25, 1988 this Court approved the class size reduction 
component for 1988-89 through 1990-91. The KCMSD has 

— A-356 — 
reviewed planned enrollments for 1991-92 and has determined 
how many teachers it will need to meet the Court-ordered 
maximum Class sizes. The budget reflecting these calculations 
was based on the assumption that base as well as them costs for 
the 1986-87 magnets will remain in the desegregation budget in 
1990-91. 
The State responds that if increase in the reduced class size 
budget is enrollment driven and provided that the KCMSD hired 
only the number of teachers that are needed to meet the court- 
ordered maximum class sizes, the State has no objection to the 
Court’s continuation of this component. However, the State 
requests that the KCMSD file a report with the DMC by October 
15, 1991 justifying the number of full-time equivalents funded 
under this component and listing actual enrollment in each 
school, teachers hired under this component and grade level for 
teachers hired. 
The KCMSD responds that the type of detailed report re- 
quested by the State is best left to discussion among the parties 
and the DMC. The KCMSD believes it can work with the DMC 
and the parties to devise a report that will not be unduly 
burdensome and that can be delivered by a reasonably early date 
in the fall of 1991. 
The KCMSD has presented a proposed method which it 
believes will more equitably allocate teacher costs between the 
operating and desegregation budgets. 
If such a method is 
approved. the budgetary estimate for reduced class size may 
have to be adjusted. The State reserves 
the right to contest such 
adjustments and the parties agree to brief this issue at the 
appropriate time.

Summer School 1992 
On July 25, 1988 this Court approved a summer school 
program for the summers of 1989-91. 
Last year the parties 
agreed to modify the 1991 summer school program. 
The 
KCMSD proposes to offer the same summer school program in 
1992. The proposed budget does not include transportation. A 
request for transportation will be filed later on an actual cost 
basis. 
The State responds that the KCMSD should provide an 
estimate of its transportation costs in order to enable to the State 
to comment on the proposed budget and asks that the Court 
require the KCMSD to present a complete budget to the Court. 
The KCMSD states that it has no means of accurately discern- 
ing, until immediately before summer classes begin, the trans- 
portation related costs of the summer program. The KCMSD 
indicated that a very rough estimate of costs would be $610,281 
— the amount budgeted in 1991 for transportation costs. There- 
fore, the State reserves the right to contest transportation costs.

be successful. The KCMSD requests a slightly increased level 
of funding based on increased enrollment for the 1991-92 school

Extended Day at Traditional Schools 
In 1988 this Court approved an extended day program in ten 
traditional elementary schools with a total budget of $600,000. 
This Court’s July 5, 1989 Order approved an extended day 
program for six schools for 1989-90. The KCMSD proposes that 
extended day should be continued at the six schools.

$453,000 
$113,250 
Effective Schools 
In the July 5, 1988 Order the Court approved the funding 
requested by the KCMSD for the effective schools component 
for 1988-89 through 1990-91. The KCMSD proposes to con- 
tinue the effective schools program under current DMC guide- 
lines and procedures with the same level of funding per school. 
However, as a result of the anticipated change in the use of 
Southeast Annex, the budget requested is below that approved 
for 1990-91. 
The State responds that there are no measures of reasonable 
progress in place which can evaluate the effectiveness of this 
program. Therefore, the KCMSD should be required to prove 
that increases in test scores are directly attributable to the 
effective schools funding. Dr. Marks requested one more year 
to show some kind of increase in test scores. 
Based on Dr. 
Marks’ promise that test scores will go up or 
he will not request 
continuation of the program, the State does not object to this 
program being continued for one additional year. The State 
urges the Court to require the development of a definite set of 
standards by which the efficacy of effective schools funding will 
be measured over the next year. 
The KCMSD states that instituting a more elaborate “evalua- 
tive criteria” process would take time and administrative re- 
it

sources. The KCMSD believes it is more productive for this 
issue to proceed on an informal basis through the DMC rather 
than formalizing the process and creating new burdens for the 
program.

Base Budgets 
For 1990-91 the Court approved desegregation funding for the 
base costs of seven new magnet schools. The KCMSD proposes 
that base costs of these schools continue to be funded in this way. 
The State does not object to this part of the Year VII desegre- 
gation plan as long as the FTE’s hired are based on actual 
enrollments. 
As with reduced class size, the KCMSD states that it is 
confident that working with the same parties and the DMC, it can 
devise a simple report that responds to the State’s needs and 
deliver the report by an appropriate date in the fall.

District Communications 
In its July 25, 1988 Order the Court approved a three-year 
budget for the District Communications office. The KCMSD 
proposes to continue this program in Year VII. 
The KCMSD resubmitted a proposal in which it agreed not to 
use desegregation funds to print an annual report. In addition the 
KCMSD agreed to freeze this budget at its 1990-91 level.

Interest Expense 
In its July 25, 1988 Order the Court found that the interest 
expense on the KCMSD’s original bond issue for capital im- 
provement costs was a desegregation expense. Therefore, the 
KCMSD has treated these expenses as part of its annual deseg- 
regation budgets and proposes to continue to do so in 1991-92.

computer equipment for KCMSD research, evaluation and test- 
ing department. The Court also approved a repair and mainte- 
nance contract for such equipment in 1989-90 and 1990-91.

The total stipulated Year VII budget is $30,915,600. 
The 
KCMSD’s obligation is $12,545,619. 
The components of the Year VII budget which are disputed 
and which were the subject of the hearing are: early childhood 
education, 1986-87 magnets, security, lengthened school day, 
Central High School coaches, Montessori equipment, TTAP for 
paraprofessionals, resource teachers and home-school coordina- 
tor for J.A. Rogers, and reconsideration of computers for 
Richardson and Banneker schools. Components of the budget 
which were not discussed at the hearing but which are disputed 
— A-361 — 
are: AAA Achievement, recruitment aide and five of six 1986- 
87 magnet schools. 
Early Childhood Education Development Program 
In its July 25, 1988 Order this Court approved the Early 
Childhood Education Development program requested by the 
KCMSD with several modifications. The KCMSD and State 
have jointly developed the Early Childhood program for 1991- 
92. However, the State has not agreed to an expansion of the 
program. The KCMSD proposes to offer the program to an 
additional 400 students next year. The KCMSD intends to locate 
the classrooms for this expansion at Pershing School. 
The 
program will provide screening for 4,464 children ages three to 
four; instructional services to 1,400 children in early learning 
center classrooms and parent education services to 5,000 fami- 
lies. 
The State objects to the KCMSD’s effort to add classrooms 
and teachers in light of the KCMSD’s current budget crisis. 
Additional classrooms and teachers would add administrative 
expenses. In addition, the State points out that the budget request 
is only part of the funding requested by the KCMSD because 
staging costs have not been included. Staging costs would be 
approximately 1.1 million making the total increase 2.3 million 
and would bring the total cost of the early childhood program to 
approximately 5.9 million. The State asserts that the KCMSD 
should not attempt to provide expanded programs at this point. 
The KCMSD replies that the increase requested is an enroll- 
ment driven expansion and is an ongoing obligation on the part 
of the State to remedy years of State-imposed segregation. The 
evidence presented at the hearing demonstrated that the Early 
Childhood program is valuable and successful in enhancing the 
achievement of developmentally delayed children. The State 
has also objected because of the cost of adding 20 teachers. 

— A-362 — 
The KCMSD notes that to the extent the State objects to the 
costs of staging the expanded program at Pershing school, these 
matters will be fully argued through the briefing process on this 
matter and that particular issue is still before the DMC. The 
evidence at the hearing supported the need for an expansion in 
the Early Childhood program to accommodate the enrollment 
demand. Therefore the Court will approve the District’s pro- 
posal to expand the Early Childhood program. 
1990-91 Budget 
1991-92 Budget KCMSD Obligation 
$3,598,491

$1,208,923 
Lincoln College Preparatory Teachers 
(1986-87 Magnet Schools) 
The KCMSD states the LCPA is a successful magnet school 
which includes an International Baccalaureate program. KCMSD 
presented evidence which shows that to maintain and improve 
Lincoln’s success, five additional teachers are necessary to teach 
the academically rigorous courses and Advanced Placement 
courses. The DMC took no exception to this proposal “provided 
that meaningful increases in the admissions standards are insti- 
tuted at Lincoln.” Dr. Henley stated in her testimony that such 
an increase would be adopted for fall of 1992. The KCMSD 
submits that it currently does not have enough teachers to teach 
the intensive IB and AP classes, thus teachers must use a 
planning period to teach these classes. The KCMSD proposes 
the addition of two resource teachers to support the IB program. 
These teachers would provide academic guidance, counseling 
and mentoring to prepare students for the IB program and AP 
classes. 
The State asserts that the mentoring teachers will serve to 
accommodate the increased admissions standards in serving 
students who are having academic difficulties. The State feels 
that the KCMSD should consider whether those students should 
— A-363 — 
be demitted to enroll at other schools. Since Lincoln College 
Prep is designed to be a school for high achievers, there should 
not be a need for the proposed remedial help that the KCMSD is 
requesting. 
The State adds that the sole reason for adding 
teachers is to give currently employed teachers planning time, 
which the State considers to be an insufficient reason at this time 
of budget crisis. 
The cost of adding five teachers is $233,017 and is included 
in the proposed budget for 1986-87 magnet schools. 
Security 
The KCMSD presented evidence that additional security is 
necessary to school safety and desegregative attractiveness. 
Captain Robert Livingston stated that there has been a 34% 
increase in personal security-related incidents and the KCMSD 
proposal is designed to address such incidents. The KCMSD 
proposes to add twenty-four site-based officers and four patrol 
officers. The proposed increase in school-based security offic- 
ers would bring the average number of security officers up to 
four officers at the high school level and two officers at the 
middle school level. 
The goal is to deter personal security 
incidents by increasing security staff visibility and providing 
more comprehensive coverage of each school facility. 
The KCMSD also proposes a pilot identification card system 
for Southeast High School which is designed to allow staff and 
administrators to quickly identify individuals involved in an 
incident to determine whether they belong in the facility. In 
addition, the KCMSD proposes hand-held metal detectors to 
provide for a quick and unintru.ive weapon search which will 
deter students from carrying weapons into the school buildings. 
The KCMSD has proposed a pilot closed-circuit TV system 
for Southwest High School. The CCTV system is designed to 
allow security personnel to more effectively monitor the build- 

— A-364 — 
ing and promptly deploy personnel to potential security inci- 
dents as they develop The KCMSD anticipates that these pilot 
programs will be more cost-effective than adding additional 
security guards beyond those currently proposed by the KCMSD. 
The State asserts that there was no showing that the KCMSD’s 
security proposal is directed at any particular security problem 
which is related to prior segregation. The State emphasizes that 
the security measures proposed by the KCMSD 
are not vandal- 
proof and Mr. Livingston, witness for the KCMSD, admitted that 
the KCMSD had not considered whether it could possibly close 
certain entrances and exits to alleviate the need for additional 
security. Nor has the KCMSD pursued any arrangement with the 
Kansas City, Missouri Police Department to provide for further 
security needs. The State submits that, in this time of budgeting 
crisis, expanded and expensive pilot security programs which 
are of speculative value, should not be approved. 
The Court will approve the District’s security proposal. How- 
ever, the Court notes that the obligation to provide security is the 
primary responsibility of the Kansas City, Missouri Police 
Department and the District is encouraged to request the Police 
Department to fulfill its obligation. 
N/A 
$1,003,345 
$250,836 
Lengthened School Day at East, Southeast, Central and 
Northeast Middle Schools 
The KCMSD proposes to lengthen the school day at four 
magnet schools to make the themes more educationally effective 
and desegregatively attractive. 
The KCMSD presented evi- 
dence that it was difficult 
to fit the required state, magnet theme 
and general elective curriculum classes into the standard seven- 
day period. In addition, lengthening the school day will allow 
— A-365 — 
students to take traditional secondary electives such as advanced 
courses in music, typing and ROTC. 
The State objects to lengthening the school day because of 
additional administrative costs and lack of a demonstrable inter- 
est in the proposal. This proposal would add 24 new teachers to 
the KCMSD for which the KCMSD has no plan to pay its share 
of the additional cost of this proposal. Furthermore, the State 
asserts that with more careful scheduling, students could take all 
of the courses needed to complete a program in a seven period 
day. The State argues that no showing was made that this 
expensive adjustment is necessary. 
The Court will approve the District’s request to lengthen the 
school day at East, Southeast, Central and Northeast Middle 
schools. 
N/A 
$929,728 
$2 32.432 
Central High School Coaches 
The KCMSD requests Court approval for additional coaches 
at Central High School, beyond those funded through the oper- 
ating budget at other schools. The KCMSD states that it will not 
ordinarily employ a coach for a team sport based upon the 
request of a single or a few students; however, for those sports 
which require only a single participant, the KCMSD will criti- 
cally evaluate the need for providing that student with a coach. 
The KCMSD specifically requests an off-season track and field 
coach. 
The State asserts that there are substantial numbers of person- 
nel already directed at teaching athletics at Central High School 
and they should be sufficient to provide any coaching that is 
needed. The State adds that it seems particularly inappropriate 
for the KCMSD to assert a demand for additional coaches unless 

— A-366 — 
there is a substantial demand demonstrated by the KCMSD from 
a significant number of students who are interested in a particular 
activity. 
The Court will deny the District’s proposal for Central High 
School Coaches. However, the District may submit separate 
motions to the Court, upon a showing of need and demand, 
requesting coaches for specific sports.

The KCMSD presented evidence that it is necessary to teach

only internationally recognized American certification. 
Montessori schools rely on special equipment instead of 
traditional textbooks. The equipment requested is necessary to 
implement the magnet theme in the six additional classrooms 
that open next year. 
All of the equipment requested is also 
required to meet the AMI accreditation standard. AMI certifica-

The Court will approve the District’s proposal for Montessori 
equipment.

find it difficult, if not impossible, to obtain a Missouri teaching

The State responds that the KCMSD proposes a cost of 
$63,000 for this program and that in addition to adding costs for 
which the KCMSD has no funds available, this new program 
proposal does not appear to lead to obtaining high quality 
teachers that the Court’s salary increase of July 23, 1990 was 
directed to accomplish. The State objects to the low grade point 
average required for the TTAP. While the State acknowledges 
that this proposal could be worthwhile in a district with funds to 
spare, it is not a program which is necessary. Clearly, the State 
submits, if this were an operating budget program, it would be a 
candidate for budget cutting. 
The Court will deny the District’s request for Tuition Assis- 
tance for Paraprofessionals based on the reasons set forth by the 
State.

AAA Achievement 
On July 25, 1988 this Court approved the AAA component 
requested by the KCMSD for 1988-89 through 1990-91 in order 
for the KCMSD to be able to provide sufficient library, counsel- 
ing, art, music and physical education services to attaina AAA 
rating. 
For 1991-92 the KCMSD has calculated its AAA 
requirements according to the same formula used in the past. 
The State asserts that the KCMSD ought to bear the full cost 
of maintenance of AAA status as an operating expense. The 
State maintains that it has fulfilled its initial obligations to fund 
the attainment of AAA status and has funded maintenance as 
— A-371 — 
well. This, the State believes that it is appropriate to shift fiscal 
responsibility for maintenance of AAA back to the KCMSD 
entirely as an operating expense. Alternatively the State sug- 
gests that this Court shift the allocation to the KCMSD 
as a 100% 
desegregation liability. The State particularly objects to the 
KCMSD using desegregation funds to maintain certain libraries 
in excess of AAA standards. The State submits that in no event 
should it be required to pay more than $1,479,100 which is the 
appropriate cost for library maintenance. 
In its reply the KCMSD notes that this Court has previously 
rejected the KCMSD’s argument. The KCMSD also disagrees 
with the State’s position that the AAA budget devoted to library 
resources is too high because this Court has previously approved 
AAA budgets built exactly the same way. 
The State notes that the KCMSD stipulated that the count of 
some materials in its libraries is in excess of the minimum 
requirements for AAA, due partly to the use of AAA mainte- 
+ 
nance funds. The State’s position is that AAA maintenance 
funds should not be used to exceed the minimum requirements 
for AAA libraries. AAA maintenance funds should not be used 
to allow the KCMSD to exceed the AAA library standards. 
Thus, the State argues that there is no basis to require more than 
| the cost of reaching the minimum requirements for AAA librar- 
ies with any desegregation funds. 
The Court agrees that the State should not be required to assist 
the District in exceeding AAA requirements for libraries. There- 
fore, the Court will approve $1,479,100 for library resources.

APPENDIX Q 
IN 
THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
Case No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al., 
Plaintiffs, 
v. 
STATE OF MISSOURI, et al., 
Defendants. 
KCMSD’S REPLY TO STATE’S RESPONSE TO 
KCMSD’S MOTION FOR APPROVAL OF 
DESEGREGATION PLAN FOR 1992-93 
The State groundlessly opposes the entirety of the Kansas 
City, Missouri School District’s (“KCMSD” or the “District’’) 
Desegregation Plan for 1992-93 based on its myopic view of the 
Supreme Court’s recent decision 
in Freeman v. Pitts,60U.S.L.W. 
4286 (March 31, 1992). The State’s narrow focus misses the 
most fundamental tenents of school desegregation law recently 
reaffirmed by the Supreme Court both in Freeman a month ago 
and in Board of Education of Oklahoma City Public Schools v. 
Dowell, 498 U.S. __, 111 S. Ct. 630 (1991), only last term. 
In both cases, the Supreme Court resoundingly reaffirmed its 
longstanding holding that defendants in school desegregation 
cases have an affirmative duty to eliminate all the vestiges of 
segregation to the extent practicable.' The State cannot bring 
' See Freeman, 
60 U.S.L.W. at 4291 (“duty . . . to take all steps necessary 
to eliminate the vestiges of the unconstitutional de jure system”), 4292 (“end 
purpose must be to remedy the violation”), 4293 (“District Court should 
address whether the vestiges of past discrimination have been eliminated to 
the extent practicable”), and 4294 (“curing the effects of specific violation”); 
r well, 111 S.Ct. at 638 (“whether the vestiges of past discrimination have 
'iminated 
to the extent practicable”). 
— A-381 — 
itself even to allege that the “system wide reduction in student 
achievement” caused by State-mandated segregation in the 
KCMSD has been eliminated, Jenkins v. Missouri, 639 F. Supp. 
19, 24 (W.D. Mo. 1985) (emphasis in original), aff'd as modi- 
fied, 807 F.2d 657 (8th Cir.) (en banc), cert. denied, 484 U.S. 816 
(1987), or that KCMSD’s student enrollment is desegregated. 
See, ¢.g., id. at 27 29, 31 and 35 (various remedial components 
ordered will help in “attracting and maintaining non-minority 
student enrollment”). Because it has not alleged, and certainly 
cannot prove, that the vestiges of segregation in the areas of 
educational quality and student enrollment have been elimi- 
nated, its argument that the Milliken I] components of this 
Court’s remedy should be discontinued or phased out is meritless. 
The objections to particular components raised in Section 
II of 
the State’s Response are similarly groundless. 
The State’s 
factual arguments are based almost entirely on misinterpreta- 
tions of KCMSD evaluation data and misreadings 
of the Year 
VIII proposal. Moreover, they conflict with the essential thrust 
of the State’s argument in Section I in significant respects and 
thereby demonstrate that the State’s primary goal is to avoid its 
obligations as a constitutional violator. 
I. The Supreme Court’s Decisions in Freeman v. Pitts 
Provides No Basis for Discontinuing Integral Aspects of 
the Ongoing Remedy in this Case. 
A. The State Cannot Meet its Burden of Proving, Nor 
Does it Even Allege, that the Vestiges of Segrega- 
tion In the Areas of Educational Quality And 
Student Enrollment Have Been Eliminated as Far 
as Practicable 
in KCMSD. 
The State’s misplaced reliance on Freeman v. Pitts rests on 
several obvious misreadings of the Supreme Court’s decision. 
The most blatant of these is the State’s failure to recognize that 
elimination of the vestiges of segregation to the extent practi- 

— A-382 — 
cable is the essential prerequisite to a declaration of unitary 
status with respect to any area of a school desegregation remedy. 
The State erroneously takes the Supreme Court’s language in 
Freeman out of context to make its claim that “the ultimate 
object” of a desegregation case is “to return school districts to the 
control of local authorities.” State’s Response at 9. The State, 
however, ignores the first statement in that same paragraph that 
“the Court's end purpose must be to remedy the violation. . . .” 
60 U.S.L.W. at 4292 (emphasis added).’ 
The Supreme Court, however, strongly reaffirmed in Free- 
man the duty and responsibility of defendants liable for inten- 
tional segregation “to take all steps necessary to eliminate the 
vestiges of the unconstitutional de jure system.” 60 U.S.L.W., 
at 4291.3 Reiterating the principles articulated in Dowell, the 
Court stated: 
2 The State’s reading of Freeman also ignores the Supreme Court's 
reaffirmation of the importance of addressing educational quality issues in 
school desegregation remedies. The Supreme Court in Freeman ruled that, 
when a federal court considers whether to relinquish supervision over part of 
a school district’s operation, “quality of education [is] a legitimate inquiry in 
determining [a school district’ s] 
compliance with the desegregation decree.” 
60 U.S.L.W. 
at 4293. The appropriateness 
of such educational relief has been 
clear since the Court’s 1977 decision in Milliken II finding that in addition to 
correcting the racial isolation that results from prior segregation, defendants 
who are liable must correct “educational deficits” as well. Milliken v. 
Bradley, 433 U.S. 267 (1977) (“Milliken IT”). 
>The Court in Freeman authorized district courts to exercise their discre- 
tion to withdraw supervision from general areas of school district operations 
only where: (1) the vestiges of segregation in that area have been eliminated 
as far as practicable; (2) the defendants have completely and satisfactorily 
complied with the court’s orders in that area; (3) continued judicial control in 
that area is not necessary to achieve compliance in other areas of school 
district operations; and (4) the defendants have demonstrated a good faith 
commitment to the court’s decree and to their duties under the U.S. Consti- 
tution. 
— A-383 — 
The District Court should address itself to whether the 
Board ha[s] complied in good faith with the decree since it 
was entered, and whether the vestiges of segregation have 
been eliminated to the extent practicable. 
Freeman, 60 U.S.L.W. at 4293 (quoting Dowell, 111 S. Ct. at 
638) (emphasis added). 
Of course, the Dowell formulation 
simply restated the Supreme Court’s repeated holdings over the 
years that effectiveness in eliminating vestiges of discrimination 
is the standard to be employed when assessing whether a school 
district, or aspects of its operations, should be declared unitary. 
See Columbus Board of Education v. Penick, 443 U.S. 449 
(1979); Keyes v. School District No. 1, 413 U.S. 189 (1973); 
ye Charlotte-Mecklenburg Board of Education, 402 U.S. 
The State thus ignores decades of consistent jurisprudence 
and asks this Court to relinquish its jurisdiction over thirteen of 
the fifteen remedial components contained in the District’s Year 
vill Desegregation Plan‘ without even alleging, let alone prov- 
ing, that the vestiges of segregation found by this Court have 
been eliminated. As the Freeman Court stated, a defendant in a 
school desegregation case who seeks to end district court super- 
vision, and thereby its own remedial obligations, “bears the 
burden of showing that any current [racial] imbalance [or other 
persistent effect of segregation] is not traceable, in a proximate 
way, to the prior violation.” 60 U.S.L.W. at 4294. Accordingly, 
in order to support eliminating these components under Free- 
man, the State must prove that the deficits in educational out- 
comes due to segregation have been eliminated in the KCMSD, 
or that any remaining deficits are not traceable to the prior 
violation. Moreover, the State must show that KCMSD has 
achieved a desegregated student enrollment to the extent practi- 
“The two remaining components, Interest Expense and High School 
cotmmabeantiilniptiiemabbanets amnntentene 
percent 
of the District’s proposed Year VIII budget. 

— A-$0t<. 
cable. The State says nothing about what its proof will show on 
these issues. In fact, the evidence will show that the damage to 
educational achievement in the KCMSD that was caused by 
sevregated schools persists, and that the programs ordered to 
date by the Court have begun to address the problem but have not 
yet fully succeeded. The evidence also will show that, while 
progress has been made in attracting a desegregated enrollment, 
particularly in magnet schools, more progress is necessary and 
achievable. 
The State has not moved for unitary status on any component 
of the remedy or indicated that it can carry its burden of showing 
that unitariness has been achieved.’ Instead, the State apparently 
is content to contend that merely because versions of these 
programs were implemented last year, the year before or for 
several years, they should be eliminated.® State’s Response at 
10. But the Supreme Court held early on in Raney v. Board of 
Education, 391 U.S. 553 (1968), and recently reaffirmed in both 
Dowell and Freeman, that a release of jurisdiction upon a 
‘The Supreme Court in Freeman

a process

eliminated 
to the extent practicable, 
that the defendants have complied with

desegregation and that the criteria for incremental withdrawal of court

6 The State’s occasional reference to the District’s ostensible ability to

showing merely that a school district adopted a remedial plan 
would “be inconsistent with the responsibility imposed on the

The State’s failure to allege that the vestiges of segregation 
have been eliminated 
to the extent practicable 
is fatal to its entire 
misguided Freeman argument. The State cannot meet its burden

raised in Section I of its brief in support of its proposal to 
eviscerate the Milliken II plan in this case should be rejected 
summarily. 
= ft) — 
B. 
Freeman Does Not Suggest that Particular Pro- 
grams Should Be Terminated As Soon As They Are 
Implemented. 
The State errs in attempting to stretch the Supreme Court’s 
holding in Freeman — that it is permissible for a district court to 
release a school district from supervision in certain broad areas 
of its school operations where the vestiges of segregation in 
those areas have been eliminated — to argue that atrial court may 
eliminate particular educational programs as soon as they are 
implemented. The Court’s opinion in Freeman, however, sug- 
gests only that it may be appropriate for a district 
court in its 
discretion to release defendants from its jurisdiction in general 
areas of school operations, such as the six Green factors’ (physi- 
cal facilities, transportation, student assignments, faculty, staff, 
and extracurricular activities) or in the area of educational 
quality. 
The State’s argument that particular programs or schools, as 
opposed to general areas of school district operation, can be 
declared unitary incrementally not only misinterprets 
Freeman, 
but would lead to absurd results; if particular programs or 
schools were eliminated from the remedy upon implementation, 
no desegregation remedy could succeed. Pushed to its logical 
limits, the State’s argument means that once a particular school 
or classroom implemented desegregation programs, it could be 
released from court jurisdiction. 
Freeman is quite clearly 
contrary to the State’s extreme assertion. 
There also is nothing in Freeman that would allow an indi- 
vidual school to be declared unitary, even where it has been 
’ The Court in Freeman referred to these six areas of school operations, first 
articulated 
in Green v. New Kent County School Board, 377 U.S. 
218 (1968), 
that a district court should evaluate to determine whether all the effects of 
prior discrimination have been eliminated and a school district therefore 
properly 
can be declared unitary. 

— A-388 — 
successfully desegregated. 
The Court in Freeman directed 
district courts to examine success in desegregation with respect 
to the Green factors and the overall quality of education in the 
school system. These areas are far broader than an isolated 
examination of an individual school or program. Accordingly, 
the State’s effort to terminate individual schools or programs 
because they supposedly are “implemented” in some sense must 
fail. 
C. 
Under Freeman, Judicial Supervision Even of A 
General 
Area of School Operation 
Cannot Be Lifted 
If It Negatively Impacts Other Areas of the School 
System. 
The State’s suggestion that Freeman provides a basis for this 
Court to abandon particular programs as soon as they are 
implemented is also incorrect because it ignores the Supreme 
Court’s insistence that district courts consider whether it is 
necessary or practicable to retain control of certain areas of 
school district operations where the vestiges of segregation have 
been eliminated, in order to ensure compliance in another area. 
60 U.S.L.W. at 4293. 
It simply makes no sense to consider 
eliminating the educational components of the Milliken II relief 
ordered in this case where the vestiges of segregation remain, 
especially in the area of educational quality. The Milliken Il 
programs form the essential foundation of the entire remedy. For 
example, the efficacy of the Long Range Magnet Plan would be 
undermined drastically if KCMSD class sizes increased substan- 
tially or the District lost AAA status as tie State proposes. Thus, 
even if the vestiges of segregation in the area of educational 
quality had been eliminated — which they have not been and the 
State does not claim otherwise — Freeman does not provide a 
basis for abandoning the Milliken II programs because of their 
integral importance to desegregation in the related area of 
student assignments. See, e.g.,639 F. Supp at 27, 29 , 31 and 35 
(importance of remedial education components “to attracting 
1 maintaining, non-minority student enrollment”). 
— A-389 — 
The State apparently fails to see the distinction between a 
school district which has been operating under continuing judi- 
cial oversight for twenty-three years (as the DeKalb County, 
Georgia School System had been in Freeman) and KCMSD, 
which is early in the process of implementing fully a desegrega- 
tion remedy that has been ordered incrementally over a period of 
only seven years. 
The State, however, does concede that the components of the 
Year VIII plan “have all been fully implemented,” and it con- 
gratulates the District for the first time on its “consistent pattern 
of lawful conduct directed to eliminating earlier violations.” 
State’s Response at 11, 17. Considering the source of this 
commendation, it is a remarkable tribute to KCMSD and Super- 
intendent Walter Marks. Only a year ago the State was accusing 
the District of “repetitious failures” in implementation, see 
Letter from Bart A. Matanic to Allen R. Snyder and Kevin J. 
Lanigan at 3 (March 28, 1991) (State’s response to informal 
discovery requests), and arguing that the District’s implementa- 
tion of the remedy was so poor that KCMSD should be placed in 
receivership. 
See State’s Response to KCMSD’s Motion to 
Expedite Ruling, Initial Responses to its Motion for Funding 
Relief and Memorandum in Support of State’s Motion to Re- 
quire KCMSD to Show Cause Why It Should Not Be Placed in 
Receivership and for Further Relief (filed February 14, 1992) 
(“Receivership Motion”). 
Indeed, last year the State claimed the KCMSD’s “failure of 
implementation” unduly prolongs the remedy, see Receivership 
Motion at 11, 13, but this year the State claims it is KCMSD’s 
“full implementation” that somehow unfairly extends the State’ s 
remedial obligations. State’s Response at 10. Both of these 
inconsistent claims are wrong; KCMSD’s and the State’s reme- 
dial obligations continue until their constitutional violation has 
been cured. The State simply fails to recognize this legal duty 
and factual imperative and thus fails to see the basic legal 

— A-390 — 
principles and procedures reaffirmed and clarified by the su- 
preme Court in Freeman, instead, the State grasps at any argu- 
ment it can in another attempt to escape or minimize its remedial 
responsibilities regardless of the continuing vestiges of the 
constitutional violation in the KCMSD. 
Il. 
The State’s Other Objections to Particular Compo- 
nents are Meritless. 
Despite the State’s candid admission that the District is doing 
a good job of implementing this Court’s remedy and the Joint 
Stipulation agreed upon by the parties after extensive negotia- 
tions to narrow the factual issues in dispute,® the State neverthe- 
less raises numerous factual objections to components of the 
District’s Year VIII Plan. Because these are meritless, they all 
should be rejected. 
For example, the State’s repeated allegations that KCMSD 
has not evaluated the remedial components proposed, see State’s 
Response at | and 20, and that the District seeks to expand them, 
id. at 10, are both demonstrably false. The KCMSD has done 
formal written evaluations on all of the desegregation educa- 
tional programs that are susceptible to that type of analysis and 
has informally evaluated the other components, as the State is 
already aware. See Attachment 2 to State’s Response 1-2 (Letter 
from Ruth Alderson to Diane Vaughn (March 30, 1992)). Asa 
result of these evaluations, virtually every component has been 
modified in some respect. Overall, these modifications lead to 
a reduction of more than $800,000 in the proposed Year VIII 
budget compared to the court-approved Budget for Year VII. 
* The parties have agreed in the Joint Stipulation (filed May 11, 1992), 
to 
compromise on the factual objections raised by the State to the District's 
Summer School program and Early Childhood Education program. There- 
fore, this reply brief does not address the factual issues with respect to those 
components, which 
now have been resolved. 
— A-391 — 
See Memorandum in Support of KCMSD Motion for Approval 
at 3, 28.” 
The State’s factual objections to particular programs also are 
meritless, reflect a poor understanding of the District’s propos- 
als, and are inconsistent with the State’s arguments that the 
programs should be discontinued based on Freeman. 
1. 
Extended Day. 
The State argues 
in Section II of its brief that the Extended Day 
Program should be discontinued because it is not attracting non- 
minority students to the District. Thus, the State inconsistently 
argues in Section I of its brief that the program should be ended 
because it is successful and in Section II that it should be 
disgarded because it is unsuccessful. 
The State’s argument that the Extended Day Program is 
unsuccessful is plainly incorrect. The State quotes language 
from the Extended Day Program Evaluation out of context to 
argue that it is not desegregatively attractive. The Evaluation 
Report clearly indicates, however, that the Extended Day pro- 
gram alone cannot reasonably be expected to draw significant 
numbers of non-minority students to desegregate a racially 
isolated traditional school, although the report and other evi- 
dence at the hearing will show that it is one factor that is attractive 
to non-minority parents and students. 
*The amount of the reduction proposed by KCMSD has increased since the 
filing of its original Motion because of voluntary reductions in the proposed 
Early Childhood and Summer School budgets pursuant to the Joint Stipula- 
tion. The reduced budgets are shown in the Joint Stipulation attached as 
Exhibit 
A to this brief. The District intends to continue its efforts to reach 
agreement with the parties on other disputed components. The District 
also 
has been examining the issue of whether the cuts to the operating budget that 
it has recently 
made for 1992-93 suggest desegregation 
budget cuts. The 
District will be discussing this issue with the parties and hope to have it 
resolved before the Year VIII hearing. 

— A-392 — 
More importantly, the purpose of the Extended Day Program 
at traditional schools was not solely to attract non-minority 
students but also to provide educational benefits. See, e.g., 639 
F. Supp at 32 (“to improve academic achievement”); July 5, 
1989 Order at 5-6 (achievement data showed “success of the 
program as required by this Court’s order of July 25, 1988"). As 
the evidence at the hearing will show, the same evaluation report 
cited by the State indicates that the program has had a substantial 
positive impact on the academic achievement of those students 
that participate in it. Thus, there is absolutely no evidence to 
support either of the State’s inconsistent arguments that this 
program should be discontinued. 
2. 
Effective Schools. 
With respect to Effective Schools, the State again argues on 
the one hand that the Effective Schools program should be 
eliminated because it has been successfully implemented, while 
on the other hand suggesting that it be eliminated because it has 
not been successful enough. The KCMSD’s far more reasonable 
approach, however, is to recognize both the program’s important 
achievements and its shortcomings and to revise it in order to 
make it even more effective. The District does not propose “an 
entirely new and different plan,” see State’s Response at 25, but 
rather has modified the program in several important respects to 
help provide for all schools throughout the District the same 
success that has been achieved as some schools. 
The State is flatly wrong in claiming that the new proposed 
plan departs from the “bottom up” structure of the original plan; 
indeed, the new plan will ensure more “meaningful participation 
by patrons, parents, teachers and administrators at the local 
school level,” see June 14, 1985 Order 
at 21, than the program 
as 
currently implemented. The evidence at the hearing will explain 
that the new proposal will increase the level of central adminis- 
tration support and site-based accountability to ensure that the 
rlans that percolate up from the bottom are both more appropri- 
— A-393 — 
ately designed, more closely related to the school’s overall 
instructional efforts, and more effectively implemented. 
The State also misreads the 1990-91 KCMSD Evaluation 
Report of the current Effective Schools program and, therefore, 
reaches wholly unjustified conclusions. For example, the State 
repeatedly cites grade-level data as classroom data, see State’s 
Response at 24-25, understating the degree of success achieved. 
Moreover, the State fails to explain the significance of the 
“objectives” it claims more classrooms should have met. /d. It 
also cites an evaluation of the Extended Day program without 
attempting to explain its relevance to the Effective Schools 
program. /d. at 25. The State also makes the incomprehensible 
suggestion that there should be some meaningful correlation 
between “the number of reporting classrooms and the academic 
grade level represented by that classroom.” Jd. Even if such a 
correlation did exist, it is difficult to fathom its conceivable 
significance. 
Finally, the State argues that Superintendent 
Walter Marks promised improved test scores and failed to 
deliver, but the State fails to mention that 1992 test data is not yet 
available to support its contention. More importantly, what the 
State should acknowledge is that the Year VIII Effective Schools 
— itself represents an important follow through by Dr. 
arks. 
Similarly, the State’s selective reading of the assessment 
component of the revised Effective Schools program completely 
misses 
the mark. The District does not seek to “replace” the 
focus of the Effective Schools program on math and reading, but 
rather to supplement it with measures which are more appropri- 
ately designed to measure school change over the short term. 
Student and teacher attendance, student discipline, school cli- 
mate and progress towards desegregation goals are very relevant 
to improving math and reading proficiency, notwithstanding the 
State’s assertions to the contrary. See State’s Response at 26 n.6. 

—_—* 
And, these measures will evaluate more accurately the efficacy 
of the Effective Schools component at individual schools. 
None of the State’s alleged reasons for opposing the Effective 
Schools program holds water. See State’s Response at 27. The 
program, as the State admits in Section I of its brief, has not 
failed. The District proposes to modify the Effective Schools 
program in a way that fully comports with the educational 
philosophy of the original program and which will ensure that it 
is better implemented at all schools. Although the Effective 
Schools proposal is not a “new program,” there certainly is no 
moratorium on redesigned efforts to eliminate the persistent 
effects of segregation in the KCMSD. 
3. 
1986-87 Magnets. 
The State’s open attempt to escape or minimize its remedial 
obligations is nowhere more clear than with respect to the 1986- 
87 Magnets. Pursuant to the joint request of all the parties, see 
Joint Motion for Stay of January 23, 1990 Order (January 31, 
1990), this Court, in its February 1, 1990 Order, stayed the effect 
of prior decisions finding that the base costs of the 1986-87 
Magnets would no longer be recognized as a desegregation 
expense.’ Twice since that Order this Court has ruled that the 
base costs of the 1986-87 Magnets should remain in the deseg- 
regation budget. See June 26, 1990 Order; July 5, 1991 Order. 
The State and Court are aware of the District’s operating budget 
Under 
the February 1, 1990 Order, the Court held, pursuant to ail the 
parties’ wishes, that pending receipt of a study that determines whether there 
“are additional resources that could be made available from KCMSD’s

Magnet base costs, 
the October 18, 1989, November 
2, 1989, 
and January 23, 1990 orders

that order did not identify funds in the operating budget adequate to support 
these schools. 
— A-395 — 
situation. The Deloitte & Touche management audit did not 
reveal operating budget cuts sufficient to support these schools; 
to the contrary, Deloitte & Touche suggested many investments 
on the operating side, to enhance the effectiveness of the District’ s 
operations. The 1986-87 Magnets have been an academic and 
desegregative success, and their continuation is a keystone to the 
success of the magnet school plan. Because funds from some 
other source are not available to support them, they should be 
considered as part of this plan. 
4. 
District Communications. 
The State offers no argument as to why this component should 
no longer be a desegregation expense. 
Informing the public 
about the progress of the KCMSD desegregation plan remains as 
important today as it was when the Court initiated its first 
remedial order. Indeed, in no small part because of the State’s 
continued opposition to almost every aspect of the remedy, there 
is a great deal more negative publicity with respect to the 
desegregation plan today 
than when it was first ordered. Accord- 
ingly, this component plays a very important role in the success 
of the remedy and should be continued. 
5. 
Security. 
The State’s position with respect to Security plainly disre- 
gards the facts. As the District informed the State at its meeting 
with State representatives on March 12, 1992, the Kansas City, 
Missouri Police Department has informed the District that it is 
unable to provide additional support at District schools. Simi- 
larly, the State simply ignores the information provided to it in 
the District’s desegregation plan, through the DMC process and 
through informal discovery about the necessity for the seven 
additional security positions and the replacement of security 
vehicles. 
Ironically, while the DMC took exception to the 
District’s security proposal because it did not go far enough, the 
State interposes a blanket objection to virtually every new aspect 

— A-396 — 
of the District’s security proposal. 
And, while the District 
proposes to provide a greater level of security for its students 
with a smaller budget, the State responds with sarcasm about the 
District’s alleged self-congratulation, see State’s Response at 
30, and with disregard for the safety of students. See State’s 
Response (arguing that since Security program has existed for 
several years it should be “released from supervision of this 
court,” i.e., terminated). As the evidence at the hearing will 
show, the security program is vital to the success of the remedy 
and should be continued. 
6. 
Special Education Compliance. 
The State argues that Special Education should receive no 
support through the Desegregation Plan, because the Special 
Education program is unrelated to desegregation, and because 
KCMSD 
specifically chose to exclude a Special Education 
component from its original remedial plan. 
KCMSD made a determination in 1985 that no historical 
record existed in the Kansas City School District indicating that 
minority children suffered identifiable deprivation in the area of 
special educational services traceable to the prior dual school 
system. 
As a result, no Special Education component was 
included in the original remedial plan. 
At no time subsequent to 1985 has KCMSD proposed that a 
specific special education program be included in the Desegre- 
gation Plan. 
Rather, as other components of the plan were 
implemented,] it became apparent that implementation of the 
plan was resulting in additional costs for students requiring 
special education services. Compliance with state and federal 
laws requires that KCMSD provide comparable “regular” ser- 
vices to handicapped students so that special services designed 
to address the handicapping condition will be an add-on to 
services the School District normally would supply to non- 
handicapped students. 
As funds spent on regular education 
— A-397 — 
increased through the Desegregation Plan, comparability re- 
quirements mandated that additional services be provided to 
handicapped students. Indeed, the U.S. Office for Civil Rights 
(“OCR”) recently required the District to offer assurances that it 
would provide comparable Art and Physical Education services 
for handicapped students at DeLano School to resolve compli- 
ance problems identified by OCR in these areas. See State’s 
Response, Attachment 1. The “:ate’s argument to the contrary 
notwithstanding, see State’s Response at 31, these special edu- 
cation services are required only because they are offered to 
regular students pursuant to the court-ordered desegregation 
plan; this comparability requirement formed the basis for the 
1987 addition of funding categorized as “Special Education 
Compliance” as a part of the Desegregation Budget. 
With implementation of the Long-Range Magnet Plan and the 
District’s efforts to attract additional non-minority students to 
the District, special education costs have increased. Kindergar- 
ten enrollment is up as a result of recruitment efforts. All new 
kindergarten students receive special education screening; the 
desegregation recruitment success increases the need for these 
services. 
The District also had attracted, as a part of the 
desegregation effort, added enrollment through private school 
and interdistrict transfers. These students represent a cross- 
section of the normal student population, and a certain percent- 
age of them naturally can be expected to become a part of the 
population referred for evaluation by the Department of Excep- 
tional Education. Some are found to require special services of 
various types; others require only evaluation. 
In either case, 
added cost is incurred. 
It is inevitable that an increase in the population base being 
served places an increased work load and increased costs on the 
Special Education service area. Even though a separate Special 
Education desegregation component 
has never been a part of the 
remedial plan, through evolution of other components of the 

— A-398 — 
Desegregation Plan there has been an impact upon KCMSD 
Special Education costs. The funding which has been in effect 
since 1987 began and has continued as a recognition of those 
costs. The State has offered no basis for discontinuing it. 
7. 
High School Prep. 
The High School Prep program is a successful program which 
provides a creative learning environment for students who have 
suffered the effects of State-mandated segregation in KCMSD. 
While the State is correct that this Court did not approve 
desegregation funding for this program this year, see October 8, 
1991 Order, it completely ignores the reason. See Order at 1-2 
(“[T]he KCMSD’s 
submission is untimely. 
The KCMSD 
should have submitted this program as part of the Year VII 
budget... .”). This program has been designed and presented in 
a timely fashion for 1992-93 school year, and the evidence at the 
hearing will show that it already has shown good results. More- 
over, the educational problems that otherwise would lead many 
of the High School Prep students to drop out are vestiges of 
State-mandated segregation in this District, notwithstanding the 
State’s assertion to the contrary. Rather than analyze the High 
School Prep program on the merits and attempt to determine 
whether it truly is an effective remedial program, the State just 
argues that any new program is per se unacceptable. 
This 
argument, like the State’s conflicting claim that all the existing 
programs should be terminated, is unsupported and illogical. 
CONCLUSION 
Last year the State argued that the District was not adequately 
implementing the remedy in this case. This year, the State argues 
that because the District is fully implementing the Milliken II 
components 
of the remedy they should 
be terminated. The State 
also claims that if this Court rejects this contention, some of 
these same components should be disapproved 
because they are 
unsuccessful. 
All of these ever-shifting arguments, however, 
— A-399 — 
should be rejected. They betray that the State’s mission in this 
case is simply to get out any way it can, without dealing with the 
need to do the job of desegregation right and to pursue the task 
to its successful conclusion. 
The District in fact has implemented the Milliken IT compo- 
nents of the remedy in a responsible manner. These programs, 
along with other aspects of the remedy, are beginning to show 
positive results. Nevertheless, lingering vestiges of segregation 
remain in the KCMSD, and the District’s serious efforts to 
modify and improve the various remedial components proposed 
for Year VIII are prudent and justified. 
For the foregoing reasons and those set forth in KCMSD’s 
Motion for Approval of Desegregation Plan for 1992-93 and 
Supporting Memorandum, and in light of the Joint Stipulation of 
the parties and the evidence to be presented at the hearing 
beginning in May 27, 1992, we respectfully suggest that the 
KCMSD Motion for Approval of the Desegregation Plan for 
1992-93 should be approved and ordered by the Court. 
Respectfully submitted, 
/s/ Patricia A. Brannan

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

KALIMA JENKINS, et ail., 
Plaintiffs, 
Vv.

Defendants. 
JOINT STIPULATION OF ALL PARTIES 
CONCERNING KCMSD’S 
1992-93 DESEGREGATION PLAN 
All parties to this case — plaintiffs, the State of Missouri 
Defendants (“State”), Kansas City, Missouri School District 
(“KCMSD”), and the AFT Local Union 691 (“AFT”) — hereby 
submit this Joint Stipulation regarding the KCMSD’s Motion for 
— 
Approval of Desegregation Plan for 1992-93 (filled March 25, 
1992). These stipulations reflect the parties’ attempt to narrow 
the factual issues in dispute at the hearing scheduled for May 27, 
1992 and reflect the agreement of all the parties. 
The State reserves its legal objections, based primarily on the 
Supreme Court’s recent decision in the case of Freeman v. Pitts, 
60 U.S.L.W. 4286 (March 31, 1992) as fully set forth in State’s 
Response to KCMSD Motion for Approval of Desegregation 
Plan for 1992-93 (filed April 20, 1992).' 
' AFT Local 
691 suppagts KCMSD’s Year VIII proposals, but with the 
following qualification; the AFT expressly reserves its arguments that the 
Court should provide no funding for any new desegregation programs or 
projects, nor any increased funding for existing desegregation programs or 
(Footnote 
1 continued on next page) 

— A-402 — 
Nevertheless, these stipulations reflect the agreement of all 
the parties: 1) that with respect to anumber of components, there 
is no other dispute between the parties and if the court rejects the 
State’s argument based on Freeman v. Pitts, the parties agree to 
the budget and scope of the program as proposed;? 2) that with 
respect to one component, no factual issues exist and the legal 
issues in dispute will be submitted on the briefs; and 3) that 
certain components will be the subject of a hearing. 
1. 
Components as to which There is No Factual Dispute 
Concerning the Proposed Budget. 
The parties stipulate their agreement that with respect to the 
following components there is no factual dispute as to the budget 
and the scope of the program proposed, other than that outlined 
in the State’s Response: 
* 
AAA 
* 
Reduced Class Size 
* 
Early Childhood 
* 
Full Day Kindergarten 
* 
Base Budgets 
* 
Interest Expense 
* 
Summer School 
* 
Maintenance 
(Footnote | continued) 
projects unless and until desegregation funding is provided to raise the 
salaries of KCMSD’s teachers and other employees to adequate, competitive 
levels and that providing salary levels adequate to attract and retain the quality 
of personnel necessary to implement the existing desegregation programs 
should be a higher priority than any other new initiatives. 
? The State, of course, reserves its right to appeal an adverse decision on its 
Freeman v. Pitts argument 
or any of the factual issues that are the subject of 
the hearing.

2. 
Additional Legal Issues Reserved by the Parties. 
The parties stipulate their agreement that the following com- 
ponent will not be the subject of an evidentiary hearing: 
1986-87 Magnets - The parties agree to submit their dispute 
as to the base costs for the 1986-87 Magnets on the briefs. 
3. 
Components as to which There are Factual Disputes. 
The parties agree that at this point there remain factual 
disputes among them with respect to the following components 
that may properly be the subject of an evidentiary hearing: 
> The stipulation of the parties with respect to Summer School and Early 
childhood is conditioned upon the KCMSD’S withdrawal of its request for 
multi-media equipment dedicated to the Summer School program and certain 
components of the Early Childhood program objected to by the State. The 
budgets in the chart above for these two components have been reduced 
accordingly and replace those originally requested by KCMSD. 

— A-404 — 
* 
Extended Day 
*

* — Security 
* 
High School Prep. 
* — District Communications 
* 
Special Education Compliance 
Additionally, factual disputes concerning the Freeman v. Pitts 
issues relating to the components addressed in Section | of this 
stipulation may arise and properly be the subject of testimony at 
the hearing. 
All the parties stipulate and agree that this Joint Stipulation is 
intended to resolve potential factual disputes about certain 
components, with the purpose of narrowing the issues requiring 
a hearing. This Joint Stipulation is not an admission that the 
components as originally presented were in any way deficient or 
an admission that further arguments could not be adduced to 
oppose certain components on which the parties have agreed not 
to raise certain factual disputes. All parties agree that post- 
hearing briefs shall be submitted no later than June 5, 1992.

UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

KALIMA JENKINS, et al.,

ORDER 
Upon consideration of the Joint Stipulation of all parties 
concerning desegregation budget for Year VIII, filed by the 
plaintiffs, KCMSD, The State of Missouri defendants, and the 
AFT Local Union 691, and in consideration of the entire record 
herein, it is hereby 
ORDERED that the Joint Stipulation is approved as submit- 
ted; 
AND IT IS FURTHER ORDERED, upon review of the Joint 
Stipulation, that rulings on both the continuation of the compo- 
nents and their scope are served pending a hearing on this matter 
on May 27, 1992. Briefs will be received no later than June 5, 
1992. 
Russell G. Clark, District Judge 
United States District Court 
Dated: May __, 1992

[24]

had

is just to be able to stop the flow of, in this case, white youngsters 
from the District, just to level that you and stop it is something 
that has not been done in very many urban settings. That has been 
done here, and I [25] think that’s progress. 
And we are beginning now to see the advantages of the plan 
in attracting back more white youngsters and we see schools 
where we are reaching that level, and I think getting into the 
community more, changing the image of the School District to 
a leadership School District, which we have worked very hard on 
this year is beginning to take hold. And people that will talk to 
us now and invite us into the living rooms probably 12 months 
ago wouldn’t have done that. 
So I believe we are on the verge of moving ahead with 
greater stride than we have in the last couple of years, even 
though some progress has been made. We must remember that

the School

got to keep balanced

if we did not have funding as it is set out in this document.

That is an

I'm sorry? 
— A-413 —

in student achievement; isn’t that correct? 
A. 
What we are using as a goal internally is that we want every 
school and we want the District to reach the national norm, the 
50th percentile as a goal from which to plan and work toward as 
we began to set the guidelines and objectives by school and 
district. Those are internal, totally internal goals and objectives 
we are trying to meet as we plan for the future. 
Q.

A. 
Yes, 
lam aware of that.

A. 
We could have chosen a different number as a goal 
Q.

since we need to be suburban comparable, but we rejected that 
because we thought that might take us too far in the future, but 
we could have set it at 92 percentile. 

— A-414 —

when that occurs. 
That’s not in my jurisdiction. 
It is in ny 
jurisdiction to set internal goals and objectives to be achieved. 
And that’s what we have done.

that’s outside of my jurisdiction to decide on. I think that’s 
something that will be argued in this courtroom, and that deci- 
sion will be made by people other than myself. 
Q.

in the event that your particular student achievement and

A. 
There certainly could be. Somebody could determine that.

A.

currently in place enhance the levels of desegregation and 
student achievement in this District. I know of none that are in 
place that I would not recommend at this point to go forward.

same trend, 33 percent of our schools at or above national norms 
in 1985. We are now at 47 percent at or above national norms 
today in this area. Again, indicating that we have made substan- 
tial progress here; but in fact still have work to do for many of our 
children.

Okay. Let me hand you KCMSD Exhibit 19. 

— A-422 —

A. 
Yes. This is a smaller representation of the same charts.

Q.

the

is that what we are doing is working, 
that it

road that we are going, and that we’|l continue to see the same 
kind of progress. 
[162] 
Q. 
Mr. Rainwater what are the vestiges of desegregation in the 
District? 
A. 
I think 
we saw that very clearly illustrated 
on the chart. We 
still have a substantial number of 90 percent or more minority 
schools. We still have, again, a substantial number of 80 to 90 
— A-423 — 
percent minority schools. I think we saw that our achievement 
levels are much lower than they should be. 
I believe all of those things in terms of our students still 
being in racially isolated schools and having [163] low achieve- 
ment levels, I think all of those things point to the vestiges of 
segregation. 
[184] 
Q. 
Letme just ask you a question, Mr. Rainwater, that I think 
will try to summarize, I will try to summarize your testimony 
here on cross-examination. 
Is it the case that the District, in your view, has done its 
upmost to comply with the Court’s order with respect to the 14 
continuing programs that are proposed for next year? 
A. 
Yes, we certainly have. 
Q. 
And I take it, Mr. Rainwater, that it’s fair to say that your 
testimony earlier indicated that in some respects you don’t 
believe that the District has yet achieved the goals of [185] those 
programs? 
A. 
That’s correct. I do not believe we have. 
Q. 
You testified earlier that you thought these programs were 
part of an integrated whole, and you had some cross-examination 
questions about that? 
A. 
Yes. 
Q. 
Can you explain more fully what you mean by these 
programs are part of an integrated whole? 
A. 
Certainly. I think that all of the programs ordered by the 
court, including every part, it’s tied together to try to desegregate 

—_ .' po 
the School District and to raise student achievement. All of those 
are necessary to depend upon each other. What I do not know at 
this time is what that specific relationship is to each and every 
single part, in terms of if you withdraw this part, how does that 
affect and roll out into every single other piece. So as anything 
would relate to the capital improvement program, for example, 
without some really indepth analysis to say okay, what happens 
when you do this, I don’t know, but I believe that all of the orders 
of the Court are totally integrated. 
Q. 
Let me ask you just a question. For example, if the District 
did not have the Reduced Class Size component of the Year VIII 
program, would that affect other components of the Year VIII 
proposals? 
A. 
Certainly. 
[186] 
Q. 
Why is that, how would that affect them? 
A. 
Well, the Reduced 
Class Size component, first of all, limits 
the number of kids that you have in the classroom. It would have 
an impact on the kind of support services that we provided under 
AAA. For example, it would change 
that number of teams. The 
more kids that you put into a classroom, the more problems you 
have academically and everything else, so you have now changed 
the role of the counselors that are funded under AAA, Imean you 
could go on and on, on and on. Each piece reacts to the other. 
Q. 
Would it affect the District's ability to effectively imple- 
ment the long range management? 
A. 
No question. If you increase class sizes to, but I haven’t 
done all of the numbers of this, but without reduced class sizes, 
you are looking at classes probably somewhere between 30 and 
40 kids and that would significantly impact our ability to attract 
people to come in from outside the District and to stay into the 
— A-425 — 
District. They are not going to leave schools where they have 
smaller class sizes to come into crowded classes that they know 
are not going to be effective concerning achievement. 
(187) 
Q. 
Has the District made the progress to the extent practical in 
achieving desegregation and improving achievement? 
MR. MATANIC: I object to the question. The witness

with the Court orders. 
MR. BENSON: I am not asking about complying with 
the Court’s orders. 
THE COURT: That wasn’t the question. 
A. I believe that we have made the kind of progress that [188]

THE COURT: I think the question that you have 
reached.

_ continue 
to make progress?

— A-426 —

[189] effect, have no effect on the LRMP, I can give you a list if

The other programs, in my view, as an implementor, the

feel safe in that school, they are not going to choose to send their 
precious children to that school. 
So the security proposal impacts whether they do that or 
not. If class sizes are not small and contribute to a strong student 
You can go on down the line with every single one of

one of those components contributes 
to allow the Long Range Magnet Plan to, in turn, [190] attract

stopped segregation, and we have begun to turn that back. Every 
— A-427 — 
one of those components is critical to allow the Long Range 
Magnet Plan to do that.
