APPENDIX W 
Transcript of Direct Examination of 
Dr. Terrance Stewart (May 28, 1992) 
*> 
* 
* 
[447] 
MR. FIELDS: Good afternoon, Your Honor. We have 
decided to switch the order of the witnesses in hopes that maybe 
we can get done this afternoon. 
TERRANCE STEWART, called as a witness on behalf of the 
State, being first duly sworn, testified: 
DIRECT EXAMINATION BY MR. FIELDS: 
Q Would you state your name for the record, please. 
A. Terrance Stewart. 
Q Welcome back to the case, Dr. Stewart. We haven’t seen you 
in a couple of years, at least in testimony. What’s your current 
employment, Dr. Stewart? 
A The Missouri Department of Elementary and Secondary 
Education. 
Q Would you summarize your - 
THE COURT: Would you put this mike on - 
Q Would you summarize your educational background for the 
Court, please. 
A Yes. Bachelor’s degree from the University of Missouri in 
general education, social studies; master’s degree from Central 
Missouri State University in general - or secondary school 
administration; a specialist degree from the University of Mis- 
souri. A specialist degree is the degree that’s required to become 

— A-440 — 
superintendent in Missouri. And then a doctorate degree from 
the University of Missouri in education with major area in 
general administration and a [448] secondary area in curriculum 
and instruction. 
Q Okay. Dr. Stewart, I believe you have a copy of your resume. 
A Yes. 
Q r 
ll show you what has been marked as State’s Exhibit 10 and 
ask if you can identify that. 
A Yes, that’s my resume. 
Q Before we get to that, could you also summarize your 
professional background for the Court. 
A I was a teacher in the Park Hill School District for two years, 
social studies teacher; principal for four years; a superintendent 
of schools for eight years, and I am now just completing my 8th 
year as an employee of the Department of Elementary and 
Secondary Education. 
Q And does State’s Exhibit 10 fairly and accurately summarize 
your professional and educational background, Dr. Stewart? 
A_ Yes, it does. 
MR. FIELDS: I move the admission of State’s 10. 
THE COURT: State’s 10 will be received. 
Q (By Mr. Fields) How long have you been involved, either 
directly or in a supervisory capacity, in the Kansas City deseg- 
regation case? 
A Since June - no, September of 1984. 
Q. Would you describe for the Court your direct [449] partici- 
pation in the case. 
— A-441 — 
A In 1984 the Court’s liability order was handed down and then 
it became the duty of the parties to fashion a remedy for the 
Court. My involvement began at that stage in the development 
of the remedy on behalf of the State. I worked with the State 
officials to develop the remedy. 
I also during that time in that capacity reviewed all the 
remedies and the plans submitted by the other parties, listened - 
sat out here in the courtroom and listened to the experts and the 
testimony that was given through the various hearings, read all 
the Court’s orders that came from those hearings. 
After the orders were - came down and we were in the 
implementation phase of the various orders, I attended all the 
MC meetings, the monitor committee meetings, and continued 
to read the various motions and the filings and briefs that came 
back and forth, continued to read all the documents from our 
department that came to the District, and continued to work with 
the District on the implementation. 
Q And in the course of that, both the development of the 
remedy and monitoring the implementation of the remedy as it 
proceeded on, did you get involved in negotiations with the 
School District officials? 
A. There were several items in the early years where we worked 
together to try to fashion or to try to work out some [450] of 
the 
differences. Particularly I think what comes to mind is the Early 
Childhood Program, but there were other issues also, so I worked 
with several of the District officials during those days. 
Q Many of those officials that you worked with still around? 
A No, they’re not. 
Q Have you also been involved in the St. Louis litigation? 

— A-442 — 
A Yes, toa lesser extent and a few less years, but, yes, probably 
for about five years. The same kind of supervisory management 
kind of responsibility. 
Q What was your title at the Department of Elementary and 
Secondary Education when you were involved in direct activity 
relating to the Kansas City case? 
A_ It was the coordinator of administrative services. 
Q And in that capacity, did you also have duties relating to the 
provision of educational services in areas of the state other than 
Kansas City? 
A Yes. The coordinator of administrative services position 
was a newly created position. 
The department wanted to 
enhance its ability within the Division of Administration to cut 
across all division lines and work with all the various divisions 
in our department. My duties - I think the reason that I was 
employed there is because of the background I had in school 
administration. They wanted [451] somebody there who was 
familiar with a wide array of educational programs, and my 
duties there, my responsibilities and supervisory responsibilities 
included working with things within the Division of Administra- 
tion, the school finance section as it pertains to the distribution 
of monies, the transportation section as it pertains to the distri- 
bution of the transportation monies, and the rules and regulations 
there, and the other sections within the Division of Administra- 
tion, the school data section as it gathered data, but the main 
responsibility really for that position was to coordinate all of the 
activities within our department as they affect and pertain to 
school districts, and that’s where the responsibility to cut across 
division lines came into play. My job was to work with the 
Division of Instruction as it worked with school districts on the 
classification program. Not so much to do the classification but 
make sure that it was a coordinated effort with the other divisions 
within our department, and the same is true for the Division of 
— A-443 — 
Vocational and Adult Education and Urban Education and the 
others. 
Q How about the Division of Curriculum Instruction? 
A Well, that’s where the classification section is, is in the 
Division of Instruction. 
Q And in the course of your duties as coordinator, did you 
became familiar with the various educational offerings that 
[452] are provided by school districts throughout the state? 
A Well, in the course of that responsibility and the one that I 
have now, part of the responsibility - maybe I could just do that, 
just jump to that. Currently my job is assistant commissioner for 
the Division of Administration. 
Q When did you get appointed to that position? 
A That was in June of ‘89. 
Q Allright. You were going to expound on your duties. 
A The Department of Elementary and Secondary Education 
has six divisions, and the divisions, for the most part, are kind of 
discrete bodies. For example, the Division of Special Education 
handles special education kinds of issues, and they don’t really 
cross lines into another part of the department. The Division of 
Vocational and Adult Education does the same kind of things 
they are all discrete within themselves. The Division of Admin- 
istration is the one division that has the responsibility to cross all 
these division lines and coordinate the administration of the 
services 
to the 540 school districts. It’s arather involved process. 
There are thirteen directors in the Division of Adminis- 
tration who report directly to me and many of them have that 
same responsibility. Now, what I mean by that, if you were to 
look on the resume, the school finance section as an example, 
they are responsible to distribute most of the monies that goes to 

— A-444 — 
school. That’s even monies that are [453] calculated and worked 
in the Division of Special Education or in the Vocational Educa- 
tion Division. 
So the staff that I am responsible for have 
responsibilities that are department-wide. 
The same would be true for the school data section. 
Those people are responsible for gathering a great deal of data all 
across the department, all across all division lines, data that has 
to do with class sizes, enrollments, anything pertaining to teach- 
ers, anything pertaining to tax rates and revenue and assessed 
valuations for school districts, a whole wide array of data that 
pertains to the curriculum of school districts and the instruction, 
the classification of the school districts, whether it’s AA or 
AAA. 
Q And this data is data that is collected by the Department of 
Elementary and Secondary Education from the school districts? 
A It’s collected from the school districts. 
It’s collected and 
assimilated in the Division of Administration, one of the sections 
over which I have responsibility. So all this is kind of a long 
answer to come back to you to say that because of those 
responsibilities and because of the people that I supervise and 
because of the documents that we have to work with, I have 
worked with virtually everybody within our department and 
every division, all the directors and become [454] knowledge- 
able of how the various programs work in the department and 
throughout Missouri. 
Q Doyoualso have legislative duties that involve the use of this 
data that’s collected from the school districts? 
A One of the items on the resume has to do with the budget. 
Well, the answer is yes. It has to do with the budget. It’s my 
responsibility to represent the Department of Elementary and 
Secondary Education in all the budget hearings in front of the 
General Assembly, whether it’s in the House Appropriations 
— A-445 — 
Committee or the Senate Appropriations Committee, and in 
order to do that and to do that effectively, and hopefully, I have 
been effective at that, is that I have to have the kinds of 
information and knowledge that pertain to absolutely every 
program that’s in the public schools in Missouri, because when 
we make a budget request, the budget request is $1.2 billion and 
then a little extra for some other things, the members of the 
General Assembly will ask whatever question they wish on 
whichever program they wish, and so I have to have the kinds of 
information and knowledge that’s necessary to be able to re- 
spond to those kinds of inquiries. 
Q As an aside, I understand the legislature has made a little 
addition to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Edu- 
cation budget that has some relevance to this case. 
A Yes, they appropriated 297.3 million for desegregation. 
[455] It’s reflected what is estimated to be the State’s share for 
both Kansas City and St. Louis. 
Q So in the course of your duties, then, both in administering 
the educational programs throughout the State and in your 
legislative duties, you are very frequently involved in requesting 
data from the various sections of the Department of Elementary 
and Secondary Education? 
A Yes. 
I receive - I request and receive a great deal of 
information on almost all the programs, and it has to be that way. 
It’s kind of hard to believe, but our budget document is truly five 
or six inches thick, just full of data that explains the various 
programs. 
; 
Q When you are speaking of various programs, you're not 
talking about programs internal to DESE. 
A No. I’m talking about all services that go to the public 
schools, to all 540 public schools. 

— A-446 — 
Q_ In the course of your preparation for testimony here, have 
you also had occasion to ask the various sections of the depart- 
ment to provide you with data? 
A_ Yes, I have. 
Q Okay. Have you also continued to be, in your role as assistant 
commissioner, have you also continued to be involved in the 
monitoring and implementation of the Kansas City remedial 
components? 
A Yes, Ihave. Inthe early days, we did not have a [456] section

the filings 
as I once

Q What is your understanding - let’s talk about AAA first. 
What is your understanding of the requirement of the Court with 
respect to AAA in the Kansas City, Missouri, School District? 
A Well, there were several requirements for that component of 
the plan. One of the requirements was to improve the library 
resources, and by resources in that sense, I really [459] just mean 
those items that are in the library that students may use, the 
books, the periodicals, the film strips. The things that are on the 
shelf. The Court asked or directed that the resources be brought 
up to meet 
and to barely exceed AAA standards for resources in 
the library, so that was one of the components. 
Along with that, the Court asked or directed that the 
number of librarians be increased, again to meet and barely 
exceed the AAA standards so that the ratio of librarians to pupils 
would be reduced from what it was prior to the Court order and 
be enhanced. 
The same would be true for counselors or the 
additional number of counselors were ordered to reduce that 
ratio and enhance the services that were provided 
by counselors. 
And then the same is true for art, music, and PE teachers, and then 
some additional teachers and teacher aides. 
— A-449 — 
Q Allright. Dr. Stewart, do you know whether the KCMSD has 
now attained AAA status? 
A Yes, they have. 
Q And do you know how that occurred? 
A They attained AAA status in all the areas in ‘85-86 except for 
the libraries and the libraries took just a little bit longer. They 
attained AAA status in their libraries in the ‘87-88 school year 
and the Court recognized both those achievements in its June “86 
order and then in its July 5, [460] 1989 order. 
Q What does full AAA status mean? 
A Well, AAA, full AAA status is reflective of the educational 
opportunities that are available in the district. It’s reflective of 
a higher opportunity in terms of number of resources that are 
available to the students, in terms of the class sizes, the pupil- 
teacher ratios, in terms of the number of counselors and librar- 
ians that can provide services to the students, and it’s reflective 
of a higher educational opportunity as compared to the lower 
standard of AA. 
Q All right. Was the Kansas City, Missouri, School District 
rated AAA prior to the 1985-86 school year? 
A Not for some years. I think they were rated AA from 1977 
to the time of the Court’s order. 
Q What does AA mean? 
A It’s reflective of the lower standards. 
In other words, it 
means there’s fewer books in the library. The pupil-teacher 
ratios are not quite as conducive to learning. They are higher. 
There may not be as many counselors available for the district.

Q Okay. Dr. Stewart, I’d like to move now to reduced class

A The Court wanted to establish maximum class sizes at the

and the opportunity for improvement and the maximum class 
sizes were K through 3 at 22, and grades 4 through 6 at 27, and 
then the secondary level, it was maximum load per teacher of 125 
students per teacher. 
Q To your knowledge, have those class sizes been attained by 
the KCMSD? 
[471]

higher. 
Q Okay. Have the reduced class sizes attained in the ‘88-89

— A-458 —

just this year.

Students? 
A It’s my understanding, yes. 
Q In your opinion, has the attainment and maintenance of the

A Yes. That’s one of the primary things that our department 
has spoken to for years is increasing the individual attention 
that’s available by a limited number of students in the class, by 
reducing class size. By reducing the class size, as the Court has 
done, the Court has assured that the opportunity for increased 
attention is available in [472] absolutely every class in the 
School District, except for those two exceptions, at every grade 
level, so the opportunity for increased individual attention to 
instruction has been assured in every school, every building, 
every grade, every class.

testified 
can’t be considered fully implemented 
until the District’ s

ing the national norms for achievement testing have been met? 
A No, I don’t believe that, either. 

— A-480 — 
Q Why not? 
A Well, there’s two parts to that. One has to do with the 60-40 
ratio, and it’s my understanding that that’s a ratio the District 
deems to be desirable or attractive or would like to have. It’s not 
a ratio that has been identified for these components, for racial 
balance or for desegregative attractiveness. As we have been 
working on implementing these components and monitoring the 
progress, we view that the goal of these components was to 
increase the opportunities for student achievement, improved 
student achievement, to enhance the potential for desegregative 
educational experiences and to enhance the likelihood of 
desegregative educational experiences by the rulings that come 
out of the Court order, but we didn’t see in there any particular 
kind of specific racial balance that was established by the Court. 
A 60-40 racial balance seems to be [499] very impractical and I 
would venture to say not at all achievable. But even setting that 
aside, it was not a goal that was available or was identified for 
these particular programs. 
Now, the other part of that question has to do with the 
testing, or the student achievement up to a certain level. I’m not 
aware that that level was established prior to the Year VIII 
budget hearing where the students would achieve at the 50 
percentile of what’s going on in the nation, the norm for the 
nation, 50 percentile, and I’m not exactly sure what the District 
means by that. Probably have to look into that. But if the District 
means the 50 percentile for every subject matter for the District, 
for probably every grade level, I would assume every grade level 
and every subject mater, that would be an extraordinarily diffi- 
cult, almost virtually impossible goal to achieve. 
National 
norms are established by using national data. Test norms are 
established by using data from students all over this country, big 
schools, small schools, rural schools, urban, metropolitan, just 
the whole gamut of schools. It would be virtually impossible for 
a school district, any school district, or in this case the Kansas 
— ft 
City School District, to be reflective of the nation as a whole. It 
would be an unachievable goal.

questions, Your Honor.

APPENDIX X 
Excerpts from Cross-Examination of 
Dr. Terrance Stewart (May 28, 1992) 
[525] 
THE COURT: 
Let me put some of this to rest. The 
Court’s goal was to integrate the Kansas City, Missouri, School 
District to the maximum degree possible, and all these other 
matters were elements to be used to try to integrate the Kansas 
City, Missouri, schools so the goal is integration. That's the goal. 
And a high standard of quality education. The magnet schools, 
the summer school program and all these programs are tied to 
that goal, and until such time as that goal has been reached, then 
we have not reached the goal. But when Dr. Stewart says no 
particular goal has been set, I think he’s in error. The goal is to 
integrate the Kansas City, Missouri, School district. So I think 
we are wasting our time. We are not wasting our time when 
we’re talking about certain segments of the goal having been 
fully implemented, but to me it indicates some degree of success, 
providing that we were correct in setting out on a program in the 
first place for [526] the purpose of integrating the Kansas City, 
Missouri, School District. Now, that’s the goal. If the Kansas 
City, Missouri School District had not been segregated, the case 
would not have been here in the first place. It’s no constitutional 
violation to not provide quality free education. If that had been 
the issue, we wouldn’t be here. If so if that gets rid of some of 
the exchange and arguments - 
MR. BENSON: 
Judge, I agree but with one bit of 
explanation. The Court also found that as a result of the prior 
segregation the School District suffered an inferior education, 
and under the Milliken 2, it had - 
— A-483 — 
THE COURT: That’s part of the vestiges and that’s part 
of the reason that we’ 
re striving to integrate. 
[$31] 
Q Dr. Stewart, do you, testifying on behalf of the State as to its 
position about opportunity, do you have an opinion as to whether 
or not the educational deficits that you acknowledged were 
vestiges of the prior segregation have been eliminated to the 
extent practicable in the Kansas City School District? 
A_ No, that’s not the purpose of my testimony, Mr. Benson. 
[$32] 
Q You have no opinion in that regard? 
A That’s not the purpose of my testimony. 
Q Do you have an opinion in that regard? 
A It’s not the purpose of my testimony.

APPENDIX AA 
Excerpts from Testimony of Dr. Walter Marks

Q Is the salary proposal that the district had before the court 
important in the district carrying out its desegregation plan? 
A Oh, absolutely. 
33,417,873 9,166,659 
44,800,000 
Q Explain your answer, please? 
35,016,077 
564,715 
10,654,870 
1,102,388 
(1) 
231,866 
261,746 
49,742,391 
(1) 
The 
prior 
estimated 
cost 
projection 
for 
equity 
adjustments 
submitted 
with 
the 
District’s 
salary 
proposal 
was 
$1,212,704. 
A_ It seems to me if you assess in a school district, the major 
areas that make a school district productive for young people, 
and that is what we are supposed to be about, is the quality of 
teaching that is being performed in the classroom. And it seems 
to me if you are not paying adequate wages to people in the 
classroom delivering that instruction to young people, you are 
going to get a teaching staff that feels to some degree demoral- 
ized. They feel inferior to other people if you are paying them 
poverty level wages. And as a result, it impacts their delivery 
system to young people. 
I think the other issue that you get into is the kind of 
conceptuality in the community, how you pay your people. And 
those usually at the bottom end of a pay scale have a different 
kind of respect from a [82] community than those being paid an 
adequate wage. SoI think it even boils over into the community. 
The last point I would make, why I think it is so imperative 
we continue this is that you also get into the issue of what people 
feel they have been promised, what people feel is a workable 
wage and what they should be paid to do that. And our teachers, 
I think, have made yeoman progress in implementing this plan.

— A-504 — 
And to have it continue at the level where we are at right now is 
absolutely crucial. And I think to remove it would cause massive 
problems in those areas. 
Q And other than teachers, which you have just described, are 
other KCMSD employees important to the desegregation plan? 
A Absolutely. As we testified the last time we were here, I think 
that all of this makes a total program, whether it is salaries, 
whether it is teachers or administrators, custodians, secretaries, 
to get this organization moving and get it to be proud of itself. 
And to really go out and be proud of what has been accomplished 
here and to help sell those accomplishments takes everybody in 
_ 
the district. It takes secretaries. People will say how do they 
impact the educational system. They’ 
re the first line of defense. 
They’re usually the people that everybody has to get through. 
And [83] it depends a lot how they relate to the public. So they 
have a integral part just like everybody else does. 
Q What will happen if the district’s salary proposal is not 
approved? 
A Well, it would roll the salaries back, as I understand it, to the 
"86-87 level. 
And that would have an effect of reducing 
beginning teachers’ salaries from I believe around 22 to 17,000. 
And it would have the same impact all the way up the line to 
teachers. 
Q Could the district pay for the salaries to make up that 
difference out of operating funds? 
A_ No, that would be next to impossible. 
Q Why do you say that? 
A WhatI found when I came to the district is that we had been 
using reserves, one time money only to balance the budget. And 
we came into this year and we had no more reserves and we had 
no more one time dollars. 
So we made the hard decision the 
— A-505 — 
operating budget had to be balanced in this district. To do that 
we had to reduce ten million dollars in that operating budget to 
balance it for next year. And that has had a major impact on the 
district. . And there just simply are no dollars available after that 
ten million dollar reduction. 
Q What is the district’s operating levy? 
[84] 
A The operating levy, I believe is $2.05, I believe. 
Q Has it been frozen at that level for a number of years? 
A Yes, it has. 
Q Dr. Marks, I am handing you what has been marked as 
KCMSD Exhibit 42. Do you recognize this? 
A Yes, Ido. 
Q What is this? 
#. This is the school district, their current tax levy in our area. 
Q How does the KCMSD levy compare now with the other 
school districts in the area? 
A Well, we are at 2.05 on this. And I believe that is the lowest 
levy that is shown on this page.

APPENDIX BB 
Excerpts from Transcript of Direct Examination 
of John Alspaugh (February 22, 1993) 
{2311} 
Q And what is your occupation? 
A I’maprofessor in the College of Education at the University 
of Missouri-Columbia. 
Q And how long have you held that occupation, Dr. Alspaugh? 
A Twenty-eight years. 
Q Do you have a specialty? 
A My specialty is educational statistics and educational re- 
search methods. 
Q Have you every evaluated student achievement data from a 
public school district? 
A Yes, I have. 
[2312] 
Q How many years have you been conducting this type of the 
student achievement evaluation? 
A_ The 28 years since I have been a faculty member I’ ve done 
this type of research. 
—_ 
Q And during that time have you ever published any articles or 
treatises and scholarly journals? 
A Yes, I have. 
[2315] 
Q Why did you switch the focus from your initial exploration 
of elementary, middie and high schools to only elementary 
schools? 
A Well, first of all, the - there is a relatively small number of 
middle schools and high schools, and all of the middle schools 
and high schools have been converted to Magnet schools, so I 
could not make Magnet versus non-Magnet schools compari- 
sons. So I did - so I dropped - or did very limited analysis on 
middle and high schools. 
Q When you did the analysis that you speak of today, did you 
reach any conclusions as a result of the research you did? 
A Well, it appears to me that there has been a decline in student 
achievement at the middle school and high school level. 
[2316] 
Q And do you have those conclusions drawn out in any particu- 
lar fashion? 
A Yes. 
Q What 
are they? 
A The overall conclusions from this analysis, basically I have 
three conclusions, the first one, there is no consistent difference 
between Magnet and non-Magnet elementary schools in terms of 
reading and math achievement. 

— A-508 — 
The second conclusion has two parts. In the first part, there 
is a difference between the socioeconomic backgrounds of 
students assigned to Magnet versus non-Magnet schools. 
Part B of the second conclusion: If you statistically remove 
the influence that socioeconomic factors contribute to KCMSD 
student achievement, then students assigned to non-Magnet 
schools tend to have higher achievement than students in non- 
Magnet schools. 
And third, the implementation of Magnet schools to in- 
crease the relative percentage of white students in the elementary 
schools is associated with lower black student achievement and 
lower overall student achievement. 
[2321] 
MR. EDELMAN: Your Honor, at this time the State would 
like to proffer Dr. John Alspaugh as an expert in statistical 
analysis. 
THE COURT: Well, he will be accepted as such. 
MR. EDELMAN: Thank you, Your Honor. 
[2330] 
* 
* * 
Q Does that mean that the children from families with less 
money tend to show lower reading and math achievement in the 
KCMSD elementary schools? 
A Yes. 
— A-509 — 
[2339] 
Q Do you consider the differences in characieristics between 
the students at Magnet schools and the students at non-Magnet 
schools to be important to this analysis? 
A Yes, Ido. 
Q Why? 
A Student achievement is highly influenced by the socioeco- 
nomic characteristics of the students, and that will have an 
impact upon the levels of achievement that I can anticipate. 
[2360] 
Q Dr. Alspaugh, would you please describe the general conclu- 
sions you draw from Exhibit No. 70? 
A ExhibitN 0. 70 is concerned with black student achievement. 
And generally it implies that black student achievement has been 
declining. 
Q Is that a districtwide measure? 
A This is districtwide. 
[2366] 
Q Okay. Could 
you 
please explain 
those data? 
you

In 1992, they were also 42 points below state average.

PHD MAXIMUM COMPARISON 
Q I 
guess based on that, you would agree, wouldn’t you, that all 
1993 PROPOSALS OF ALL PARTIES 
other things being equal, a teacher is more closely connected to 
a child’s education than is a grounds keeper at the child’s school, 
wouldn’t you? 
A Mr. Edelman, I don’t want to get in the business here of 
judging between the teacher and a grounds keeper. The teacher 
serves a very specific role and is very important to education. 
And for a district that hasn’t given teachers pay increase, the 
State hasn’t supported pay increase for teachers in the last three 
years, I don’t know whether you have the right to even talk about 
the difference between a grounds keeper and a teacher. 
But since you’ve asked me, I think the grounds keeper is 
very important because we must go out there and make sure that 
the people who come to these schools can see the school is well 
maintained and is well kept and it is safe and it is an important 
environment for children to learn. With the violence that goes on 
in schools today, with the problems that go on, every single 
employee of that school district is important in the scheme of 
things to make sure that parents choose to put their children in 
[221] public education. 
Q I think I understand 
the depth of your feeling on this issue, but 
I guess I’m not sure if I heard you state in an area where tough 
choices have to be made which Position as between a teacher and 
a grounds keeper you feel is more closely related to the child’s 
education? 

— A-526 — 
Do you have a priority on that issue? 
A I want teachers to have the ability to address the needs of 
children. 
I want teachers not to have the child confused or 
frightened or concerned because of some element in the school 
environment. 
[252] 
Q You wouldn’t support a desegregation funded pay increase 
if it were targeted only at teachers and instructional personnel, 
would you? 
A I think it would miss a very important component of the 
education order of the Court. 
We have here an order that 
addresses the physical facilities, the programmatic offerings for 
their children 
and the staff. And I think all three share an 
importance, and I don’t know how you separate them out.

APPENDIX II 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al., 
Plaintiffs, 
vs. 
STATE OF MISSOURL, et al., 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
(Filed: Jan. 10 1994] 
This matter is before the Court on the State’s motion to alter 
or amend this Court’s Order of August 9, 1993. 
Both the 
KCMSD and the plaintiffs have filed responses in support of the 
State’s position on this issue. Additionally, this matter was one 
of the issues considered in a three day hearing November 15-18, 
1993. After considering the arguments presented by the parties 
and the evidence and information available to the Court, it is the 
decision of the Court that the DMC be permitted to proceed with 
supervision of the implementation of the ShareNet Program. 
On August 9, 1993 the Court considered the proposal of the 
DMC with respect to the ShareNet program. The Court reviewed 
the program and determined that there was a sufficient basis for 
the continued exploration of the benefits available from the 
implementation of the program. As a result, the DMC forwarded 
to the Court a proposed budget and recommendation 
for the 
adoption of the program by the Court. The DMC, by resolution 
dated September 21, 1993, approved 
the recommendation of the 
— A-567 — 
VIDT Subcommittee 
in a unanimous vote. The motion 
of the 
VIDT Subcommittee read as follows: 
The DMC moves that the budget prepared by ShareNet for 
implementation 
of the VIDT plan approved by the Court on 
August 
9, 1993 be forwarded to the District Court for 
approval. The DMC anticipates 
that the requested budget 
allocations for this program will decrease as start-up equip- 
ment and initial curriculum development costs are reduced 
or eliminated, and additional cost efficiencies are devel- 
oped. 
Subsequently, the DMC has submitted to the Court a revised 
budget, attached hereto, which included additional equipment 
expense for the KCMSD but reduced the overall budget by 
approximately $100,000. 
The parties presented evidence at the hearing in opposition to 
the ShareNet proposal. However, the Court did not receive 
evidence from the DMC at the hearing. The Court finds that it 
is appropriate to consider the discussions and support of the 
DMC as substantive 
evidence in favor of the DMC proposal. 
The procedure used to bring this matter before the Court is 
unique and attributable to the nature of this case. There 
is no 
proponent of the DMC’s proposal other than the committee 
itself. As the DMC is an arm of the Court, the result is that the 
Court has brought forth a proposal for a program to be imple- 
mented as a part of the remedial plan and this proposal finds no 
support among the parties to the litigation. The Court finds that 
it has the inherent authority to shape a remedial plan in manner 
required to address the effects of segregation and that the 
ShareNet program is an appropriate method of so doing. 
The Court has carefully analyzed the suggestions in opposi- 
tion to this proposal filed by each of the parties in this case. The 
Court notes that the evidence presented at the hearing on this 
matter closely tracked the parties’ suggestions. However, 
the 
Tl 
i 
i 
i 
el 
Fee oe ed ks 
be 
ate 
nt 
te 
el 
ei eee 
— 2 

— A-568 — 
Court does note that the AFT had no particular opposition to the 
program nor did it voice any particular support. The Court has 
determined that the manner in which this proposal ultimately 
came before the Court may well have had substantial bearing on 
the ultimate alignment of opposition. 
The plaintiffs voiced 
concern that the ShareNet plan was not a bona fide voluntary 
interdistrict transfer plan as contemplated by the Eighth Circuit. 
Therefore, plaintiffs argued that the Court should reject ShareNet 
in favor of their North Kansas City VIT proposal. The Court 
never contemplated that ShareNet and North Kansas City were 
either/or proposals. Rather, the ShareNet plan offers opportuni- 
ties for the children of both the KCMSD and the participating 
suburban districts to interact both via computer and in joint field 
trip and summer camp settings. The Court believes that the 
timing of the consideration of the ShareNet proposal was a 
significant factor in both the plaintiffs’ and the KCMSD’s 
position on this plan. 
The KCMSD has nothing to lose and everything to gain from 
participation in the ShareNet program. The argument that there 
is not sufficient time to participate in the program is specious at 
best. The Court points out to the District that when it develops 
new ideas and concepts for a desegregation funded program 
there are an infinite number of possibilities for implementation. 
The Court believes that there was too much consideration given 
to the either/or argument in favor of the North Kansas City plan 
and it resulted in the District’s opposition to the ShareNet 
proposal. The District, in hopes of the Court’s adoption of the 
North Kansas City plan, actually requested that the Court defer 
not deny the ShareNet program. 
The Court has carefully 
examined the ShareNet proposal and finds that it does offer 
opportunities which are beneficial to the remedial plan in the 
KCMSD. 
The State’s opposition to the ShareNet proposal is not at all 
unexpected. The State had sponsored a VIT plan as did the 
PP 
wr» 
ll 
at i 
eS 
te 
a 
— A-569 — 
plaintiffs. While the Court does not believe that the State’s plan 
suffered from the same infirmities that the North Kansas City 
plan did, the Court’s acceptance or rejection of the State’s plan 
has absolutely no bearing on the Court’s ultimate approval of the 
ShareNet program. The Court is not surprised that the State 
would be opposed to spending tax dollars for desegregation in 
the KCMSD as this has been a consistent position of the State. 
Nevertheless, the Court finds that the arguments presented by the 
State both through briefing and through evidence are not persuasive. 
As the Court pointed out before, the ShareNet plan is before 
the Court in a most unusual procedural posture. The Eighth 
Circuit has made clear that a proper role of the DMC is to gather 
information and where possible develop programs 
that meet the 
goals of the remedial program. Jenkins v. State of Missouri, 981 
F.2d 1009, 1015 (8th Cir. 1992). The Court finds that there is 
sound basis for the approval of the plan. Common 
sense dictates 
that where suburban and KCMSD students come together there 
will be increased opportunities available. The opportunity for 
interaction through computers is a positive step to the desegre- 
gation program. This Order is not to be interpreted 
as an 
endorsement of the ShareNet plan as the model for a Voluntary 
Interdistrict Transfer plan as contemplated 
by the Eighth Circuit. 
However, it is the opinion of the Court that the DMC has come 
forward with a program that offers the possibility of enhancing 
both the desegregation goal of the remedial plan as well as the 
possibility of providing a positive educational experience. Both 
the plaintiffs and the District have brought to the Court countless 
numbers of programs in the hopes that such programs will meet 
the aforementioned 
goals. The Court finds that while ShareNet 
is not a model VIT plan it does offer sufficient desegregative and 
educational opportunities for the Court to order it funded as part 
of the remedial 
plan. 
The Court will order the funding and implementation of the 
ShareNet plan as proposed to the Court and in accordance with 

— A-570 — 
the amended budget attached hereto. The Court will further 
order that the KCMSD comply with this Order and participate in 
a good faith manner in this program. The KCMSD shall set up 
a selection system for participation in the program whereby the 
students at the selected schools will have the opportunity for 
voluntary participation. 
Moreover, should the participating 
suburban districts decide to discontinue participation in the first 
three years they will be responsible for reimbursing the State for 
the acquisition cost of the equipment. If the KCMSD discontin- 
ues participation in the program, the suburban districts will be 
allowed to keep the equipment supplied under the program but 
the KCMSD will be required to reimburse the State for the 
acquisition cost of said equipment. Additionally, if either the 
suburban district or the KCMSD withdraw from participation in 
this program in the first three years, the Court will consider a 
motion for reimbursement by the State for such other costs of 
implementation as the Court may deem appropriate. After the 
first three years, should the suburban district elect to discontinue 
the program, they will be required to return the equipment to the 
State or reimburse the State the then current value of the 
equipment. Lastly, the Court finds that while this is not neces- 
sarily a model VIT plan as contemplated by the recent Eighth 
Circuit opinions, it is 
a program along those lines and will order 
the State to fund 100% of the cost of the program. The Court will 
order that the State implement a draw down procedure in 
accordance with those currently used by the State in order that 
the expenses incurred as a result of this Order be appropriately 
The Court believes that the ShareNet plan offers important 
opportunities to both the children of the KCMSD and the 
suburban school districts. This is an important first step in the 
renewed relationships between these entities. If the remedial 
Plan is to have the success envisioned by this Court it is 
imperative that a VIT plan be a part of its. While ShareNet is not 
the model VIT, it is 
the beginning and has the effect of 
providing 
— A-571 — 
enhanced multi-cultural as well as educational opportunities to 
the plaintiff school children. The Court suggests to the parties 
that they lend their support to this program and build relation- 
ships with the suburban districts to ensure future opportunities 
for reasonable VIT plans. 
Accordingly, it is hereby 
ORDERED that the State’s motion to alter or amend this 
Court’s Order of August 9, 1993 regarding ShareNet is denied: 
and it is further 
ORDERED that the ShareNet proposal forwarded to the Court 
by the DMC is approved as set out herein; and it is further 
ORDERED 
that the State is to fund 100% of the cost of 
implementation of the ShareNet plan in accordance with the 
budget attached hereto; and it is further 
ORDERED that the KCMSD is to participate in good faith in 
the ShareNet program and the KCMSD is to set up a voluntary 
selection system at the participating schools. 
/s/ Russell G. Clark 
RUSSELL G. CLARK, 
SENIOR JUDGE 
UNITED STATES 
DISTRICT COURT 
Date: January 10th, 1994

JOHN R. GIBSON, Senior Circuit Judge. 
The State of Missouri again appeals, the second time in less 
than a year, an order of the district court' approving Milliken IP 
quality education programs. Jenkins v. Missouri, No. 77-0420- 
CV-W-4 (W.D. Mo. July 30, 1993). 
In this appeal of the 
programs ordered for the school years 1993-94 and 1994-95, the 
State asserts the same argument that we rejected with respect to 
the 1992-93 school year 
in Jenkins v. Missouri, 11 F.3d 755 (8th 
Cir. 1994) (Jenkins IX) (as amended on denial of rehearing), 
rehearing en banc denied, No. 90-2238 (8th Cir. Mar. 15, 1994). 
The State made only minor additions to the record the district 
court had already considered in earlier orders relating to the 
quality education issues and to the argument that these programs 
should be declared unitary under Freeman v. Pitts, 112 S. Ct. 
1430 (1992). The State also argues that one component of the 
'The Honorable Russell G. Clark, Senior United States District Judge for 
the Western District of Missouri. 
? Milliken v. Bradley, 433 U.S. 267 (1977). These programs included 
reduce class size, attainment and maintenance of AAA status, full-day 
kindergarten, early childhood education, extended day, effective schools, and 
district communications. 

— A-580 — 
quality education programs, the funding for the full-day kinder- 
garten program, has been rendered redundant by the passage of 
Missouri Senate Bill 380, affecting the school foundation for- 
mula. We affirm the district court’s order and its decision on the 
quality education programs. 
When KCMSD moved to continue the Milliken II quality 
education programs for the school years 1993-94 and 1994-95, 
the State raised the Freeman v. Pitts argument, as it had with 
respect to the 1992-93 year. The court held a one-day hearing. 
KCMSD presented very abbreviated testimony from Dr. Arthur 
Rainwater that his earlier testimony’ about the Milliken II quality 
education programs was unchanged--that there was substantial 
possibility for further improvement, and that the systems were 
working well. Cf. Jenkins IX, 11 F.3d at 765 (summarizing Dr. 
Rainwater’s similar testimony at earlier hearing). The State 
presented no live witnesses on this issue, but filed a declaration 
from Dr. Terrance Stewart, essentially reiterating his testimony 
from the earlier hearing. 
Cf. Jenkins IX, 11 F.3d at 764-65 
(summarizing Dr. Stewart’s testimony at earlier hearing). 
The district court in an order entered July 30, 1993 approved 
continuation of the Milliken II quality education programs for 
’ The district court held an extensive hearing on approval of the Milliken 
[I quality education programs for the 1992-93 year, which we have described 
in Jenkins IX, 11 F.3d 
at 764. While KCMSD’s motion to renew those 
programs was pending, Freeman v. Pitts was decided. The State then asked 
that the Milliken I] quality education programs be declared unitary under the 
approach articulated in Freeman and Board of Education v. Dowell, 
498 U.S. 
237 (1991). On June 17, 1992 the district court ordered continuation of the 
Milliken II programs for the 1992-93 year, and we affirmed. See Jenkins IX, 
11 F.3d at 760. We cited as additional support for the district court’ s order its 
April 16, 1993 consideration of the Freeman issue in 
adopting the long range 
magnet renewal plan, and also the district court’s July 30, 1993 order 
concerning 
the Milliken II programs. Jenkins IX, 11 F.3d at 761-62. We have 
ordered, on the State’s motion, that the record before the court in Jenkins IX 
be made a part of the record before the court in this appeal. 
— A-581 — 
the school years 1993-94 and 1994-95. 
The district court 
articulated its reasons for rejecting the Freeman v. Pitts argu- 
ment in some detail. 
The district court found the State’s 
arguments that these components were unitary to be unpersua- 
sive. While the quality education components had been partially 
successful, they had not yet met the goals in their entirety. The 
State appeals again, relying on the same arguments it advanced 
in Jenkins IX. 
I. 
In Jenkins 
IX, we affirmed 
the June 17, 1992 order concerning 
the Milliken II quality education programs for 1992-93. We 
stated: 
The only evidence before the district court with respect 
to the degree of progress on elimination of vestiges of past 
discrimination 
was at best that a start had been made. The 
evidence 
on the record fell far short of establishing 
that such 
vestiges had been eliminated to the extent practicable. 
These tests, articulated in Dowell and Freeman, answer the 
State’s argument that establishment of the programs by 
compliance with the court order is all that is required. 
“At the time of our decision in Jenkins IX, we had before us that portion 
of the July 30, 1993 order dealing with salary issues, but the portion of the 
order relating to Milliken II programs had not been appealed. See 11 F.3d 
at 
762. After the district court denied the State’s motion for reconsideration on 
October 
20, 1993, the State appealed on the Milliken II issues. During 
argument of Jenkins IX the parties did not refer to the July 30, 1993 order, nor 
was it mentioned in the arguinent of Jenkins v. Missouri, 13 F.3d 1170 (8th 
Cir. 1993) (Jenkins X). In Jenkins IX we discussed the Milliken II quality 
education portion of this order insofar as it “fleshes out the meaning of the 
earlier district court orders.” 11 F.3d 
at 762. 

— A-582 — 
The State did not try to prove that it has demonstrated a good 
faith commitment to the whole of the court’s decree. 
11 F.3d at 765 (citations omitted). 
The record now before us relevant to the Freeman issue has 
been supplemented to only a minor degree since the Jenkins IX 
appeal. Examination of the record before the district court when 
it made its order of July 30, 1993, as well as a review of the record 
made before its June 17, 1992 order, demonstrate the validity of 
our conclusions in Jenkins IX: that the State did not try to prove 
a history of good faith commitment to the whole of the court’s 
decree; that the only evidence before the district court was that 
at best a start had been made; and that the evidence fell short of 
establishing that the vestiges of past discrimination had been 
eliminated to the extent practicable. 11 F.3d at 765. The State 
essentially repeats its arguments in Jenkins IX and argues that 
Jenkins IX was wrongly decided. We reject the State’s argument 
on the merits. The court en banc has denied rehearing in Jenkins 
IX, see Jenkins v. Missouri, No. 90-2238 (8th Cir. March 1, 1994) 
(denying rehearing en banc). Jenkins IX governs our decision in 
this case on a record that is essentially identical on the Freeman 
issues. 
There is still further reason why we must affirm. As our earlier 
opinion reflected, and as the record before us in this appeal 
demonstrates, the position of the State is that having imple- 
mented and funded these Milliken II programs for a seven year 
period, its obligation should end.’ There can be no doubt that this

If the State’s claim carries with it the unstated idea that 
having put the elements of a quality educational program in

Before the district court on the motion which led to Jenkins IX, the State

receive a quality education is now permanently available to 
each student in the KCMSD, and is thus available to erase, 
if the opportunity is reasonably pursued, the vestiges of past

No. 90-2238, slip op. at 26 (8th Cir. March 15, 1994) (Beam, J. 
dissenting, joined by Bowman, Wollman, Loken and Morris 
Sheppard Arnold, JJ.). 
The State’s position has simply been that its obligation for 
these programs is concluded and, in the colorful wording of the 
dissent, that it should “now take its money and exit, stage left.” 
Id. This is precisely the meaning of the State’s argument that its 
responsibility and obligation should be terminated. 
This has 
been rejected by the judges voting to deny rehearing en banc, and 
most tellingly by the five judges joining in the dissent to denial 
of rehearing. 
Even when the State makes its alternative argument that if the 
programs are not terminated, they should be phased out gradu- 
ally, the result would be increased financial burden on KCMSD. 
This would raise a serious possibility of substantial elimination 
or disruption of the quality education programs. 
Again, the 
dissent from the denial of rehearing explicitly stated in Jenkins 
IX that “equal opportunity to receive a quality edcation” should 
be “permanently available to each student in the KCMSD.” Id. 
at 26 (emphasis added). 
So far as the State is concerned, the crux of its responsibility 
for the Milliken II programs is funding. KCMSD must admin- 
ister and operate these programs. The State seeks termination of 
the obligation to fund its share of the cost of these programs. The 
problem that remains, as the district court has been so mindful 
during the course of 
this litigation, is the KCMSD’s ability to 
fund these programs.

— A-585 — 
As we mentioned in Jenkins IX, the district court has called for 
the parties to submit plans for financing “the magnet schools and 
the entire school district at a level commensurate with the current 
funding or at least at a level where the same quality of both 
facilities and instruction may be maintained,” assuming with- 
drawal of court-ordered fundings within three, five, seven and 
ten years, alternatively. Order of April 16, 1993, slip op. at 21. 
The court’s order has thus focused sharply on the Freeman- 
Dowell issues. The language of the district court’s order in 
calling for such proposals is sufficiently broad to cover both the 
phase-out of the magnet school program funding and the Milliken 
II funding. 
We are aware that the parties have now made filings with the 
district court pursuant to the Aprill6, 1993 order, and we 
presume that the district court will in time conduct hearings on 
these issues. In Jenkins IX, we outlined many of those consid- 
erations: 
Among the factors which must inform the sound discretion 
of the court in ordering partial withdrawal are the follow- 
ing: whether there has been full and satisfactory compli- 
ance with the decree in those aspects of the system where 
supervision is to be withdrawn; whether retention of judi- 
cial control is necessary or practicable to achieve compli- 
ance with the decree in other facets of the school system; 
and whether the school district has demonstrated, to the 
public and to the parents and students of the once disfavored 
race, its good faith commitment to the whole of the court’s 
decree and to those provisions of the law and the constitu- 
tion that where the predicate for judicial intervention in the 
first instance. 
11 F.3d at 763-64 (quoting Freeman, 112 S. Ct. at 1446 (empha- 
sis added in Jenkins 
IX)). We framed the inquiry as outlined in 
Dowell: 

— A-586 — 
(T]he Supreme Court stated that in determining whether 
there was constitutional compliance in a school desegrega- 
tion case, the district court “should address itself to whether 
the Board had complied in good faith with the desegrega- 
tion decree since it was entered, and whether the vestiges of 
past discrimination had been eliminated to the extent prac- 
ticable . . .” 
Jenkins IX, 11 F.3d at 763 (quoting Dowell, 498 U.S. at 249-50). 
The State argues that it has demonstrated its good faith 
commitment to all of the court’s decrees, and that its objection 
to numerous aspects of the desegregation program in litigation 
does not negate such commitment or reflect an unwillingness or 
intransigence toward achieving the goal of providing equal 
educational opportunity. 
It argues that it has clearly met the 
Supreme Court standards for good faith commitment. 
In es- 
sence, the State is asking this court to determine an issue which 
the Supreme Court in Dowell and Freeman has made clear is an 
issue for factual determination by the district court. As we said 
in Jenkins IX, and as the record reflects in the appeal before us, 
the State has not attempted to make a factual showing on this 
issue for determination by the district court. 11 F.3d at 765. This 
issue must first be developed by the district court. 
When the district court considers the Freeman and Dowell 
issues drawn into sharp focus by its order 
of April 16, 1993, and 
now 
iefed in 
detail by the parties, we are satisfied that the court 
will be mindful of its responsibility to make detailed fact- 
findings as it considers the several inquiries required by Free- 
adequate record before us for our review. 
— A-587 — 
Il. 
The State also argues that passage of Missouri Senate Bill 380 
requires an adjustment in the State’s desegregation liability. 
Senate Bill 380 counts a full-time kindergarten child as one 
student for purposes of computing the foundation formula 
aid for 
school districts in Missouri, whereas the earlier formula counted 
such children as one-half a student. The State currently pays part 
of the cost of full-day kindergarten as a Milliken II quality 
education cost. As a result of the combination of Senate Bill 380 
and the State’s Milliken II obligations, the State argues that 
KCMSD will receive payments greater than the total cost of the 
full day kindergarten program, and that this is a windfall. 
It must first be recognized that the funding under Senate Bill 
380 measured by full-day kindergarten students need not be 
spent on kindergarten programs. It is simply a way of measuring 
allocations of general state aid that can be used for any school 
purpose. 
The district court recognized that the future impact of Senate 
Bill 380 was far from certain. The State’s witnesses testified that 
the exact amount KCMSD would receive could be significantly 
different due to a change in the method by which all students are 
counted, with the new system relying more heavily on daily 
attendance than the old system. There is a gradual phase-in of 
Senate Bill 380 formula in increments of 25% over a four year 
period, 
and the new formula would 
not be entirely implemented 
until the 1996-97 school year. Further, 
the appropriation process 
could increase or decrease the amount of money available, as 
could the amounts withheld for growth. In addition, there are 
uncertainties 
due to the state finance litigation. 
These uncertainties are reason enough for affirming the dis- 
trict court’s decision to reject the State’ s argument. Exactly what 
KCMSD may receive and when it will receive it because of 
change in the foundation formula is yet to be determined. 

— A-588 — 
The State has not demonstrated that the district court made 
findings of fact that were clearly erroneous, or abused its 
discretion in rejecting the State’s request to allow an off-set of 
the full-day kindergarten monies to reflect amounts received 
under Senate Bill 380. 
We affirm the order of the district court.

— A-592 — 
“SHOW ME-Missouri”’ 
Proposal 
INFORMAL PRELIMINARY PROPOSAL 
ADDRESSING 
VOLUNTARY STUDENT TRANSFER IN 
METROPOLITAN KANSAS CITY 
Developed and presented by: 
The participating Missouri School Districts 
of the 
ShareNet Association 
April 28, 1993 
— A-593 — 
“SHOW ME-Missouri” 
This preliminary proposal is submitted by 
over two-thirds of the participating Mis- 
souri School Districts who are members of 
the ShareNet Association. (At this time all 
indicators support continued district involve- 
ment with the potential of more districts 
joining in the plan as it is further developed 
and refined.) 
“SHOW-ME-Missouri” is: An Intrinsically Equitable, Aca- 
demically Challenging, Technology 
Assisted Program for Grades 
6-13, Resulting in an Associate of Arts Degree-- 
A program of 
this magnitude and change requires allies and 
partners. “SHOW ME-Missouri” encourages collaborative re- 
lationships among teachers, administrators, community leaders, 
college faculty, policymakers and associations. We know that 
significant and lasting change takes time. “SHOW-ME-Mis- 
souri” is a thoughtful and deliberate approach measured in 
multiple years or even a decade. Today’s students will receive 
some benefit before they graduate, but future generations will 
receive the full benefits. 
MISSION STATEMENT 
“SHOW-ME-Missouri’s Mission: 
The grand challenge for the educational! community created by 
the “SHOW ME-Missouri” Project is: 
* — to help students build themselves into equitable per- 
sons and academically skillful students through an 
innovatively structured, technology supported, edu- 
cational design 
* 
to provide, through the joint efforts of the 
colleges, 
the K-12 public school environment 
and the 

—_S 
community, a resource-rich, educationally sound, 
multi-district project that is a bias-free student cen- 
tered program 
GOALS 
* 
To provide a new learning community which is col- 
laborative and free from the barriers created by preju- 
dice and discrimination 
* 
To shape in each student the beginnings of lifelong 
learning, providing experiences and guidance that 
promote the skill and confidence to continue that 
process, even as technology continues to change the 
ways learning is offered and accessed 
* 
To establish learners who can move effectively and 
naturally among the tools of technology, in the service 
of their knowledge and growth 
BACKGROUND 
Our students have a critical need: to establish their character and 
world-view in an educationally effective environment which 
assumes and demonstrates equity, sensitivity and justice by 
invalidating racial, district, ethnic, religious or other artificially 
imposed barriers. These problems and attempts at their resolu- 
tion have been the goai of many sincere efforts to bring about that 
climate of genuine equality within the educational environment 
of the Kansas City, Missouri School District. 
Our students have another critical need: to gain technological 
literacy in this century - and the next. This literacy 
will be the 
portal to workplace and civic power. It has been said that very 
soon technological literacy will constitute a new watershed, the 
literate side of which will be those with power and the non- 
literate side those who have no means to enter the mainstream. 
It would be ironic to free students from one kind of discrimina- 
tive oppression only to deliver them into another. 
— A-595 — 
This proposal seeks to obviate both dangers. The “SHOW 
ME- 
Missouri” program creates an environment in which ethnically 
mixed students are empowered to learn in a setting of equity, 
together seeking - through academics and technology - paths to 
their place in the world. Through these new educational 
strat- 
egies and tools, students will come together in benign neutrality; 
learning to the limit of their personal horizon but actively 
needing inquiry and research interactions with their peers on the 
learning team as they examine issues of mutual importance. 
Increasingly, this same teaming relationship is basic to the 
workplace for which students are ultimately preparing. 
PHILOSOPHY 
This strategy recognized many new features. Individual stu- 
dents learn faster, with more passion and with more retention, as 
they master concepts, in depth, from many academic disciplines 
while they are learning around a broad theme (Thematic Learn- 
ing). 
Technology is a prejudice-free teaching tool, around which new 
approaches to education have been created for this program that 
are oblivious to the personal traits or race of the user. It is a 
medium that treats everyone the same, rewarding learning, 
intellect, focus, skill, and collaborative work - while indifferent 
to all else. 
Teachers who are able to “team” their professional disciplines 
deliver a more effective and compelling learning experience to 
students (Multi-Disciplinary Team Teaching). Much teacher 
effort and student frustration stem from the attempt to keep 
students learning at the same pace. The power of computing 
technology can release them from that artificial pattern (Indi- 
vidualized Learning). 
Time blocks in which this kind of learning goes forward are 
variable and often much longer than the standard fifty minute 

— A-596 — 
class period, a potentially complicated mechanical problem 
which is also well served by using computer technology. (Flex- 
ible Scheduling) 
A new style of “grading” is inherent in this new kind of educa- 
tion, so the products of student inquiry and research are collected 
as evidence of the student’s building 
skill and conceptual growth 
in each traditional academic discipline. Artificial standards are 
no longer employed. The ability to memorize is no longer as 
important as the quality of understanding and application, as 
demonstrated by the products of student work. (“Portfolio As- 
sessment”) 
A program utilizing these assumptions will soon produce stu- 
dents who were ready to do work considered “college level” 
while still in their high school years (Dual Credit, high school/ 
junior college) 
PROGRAM 
Students will enter the program at the middle school - 6th grade 
- level. They will work in learning teams in which the members 
are purposely chosen and combined to reflect member diversity. 
For instance, matching students from an inner city school with 
the different cultural mix of suburban students in the same work 
teams will achieve this balance. From the classes that would 
have been in the traditional system, a new “family” of diverse 
learning teams will be created. These “SHOW ME” scholars 
teams will be guided through academic pursuits of challenging 
inquiry learning. 
These students will experience their learning in academically 
stimulating thematic topics, the content of which will also 
prepare them to work in their learning teams. Themes will 
include: systems, comparative cultures, teaming relationships, 
— A-597 — 
As appropriate, students will also have guidance from commu- 
nity mentors or leaders with expertise in fields of endeavor that 
parallel their studies. They will, in “field trip” contexts, meet 
together at strategic community sites (community colleges, 
businesses and cultural locations). These meetings are held to 
meet the needs of the students by broading [sic] the scope of their 
thematic learning. 
This program is educationally driven, however the students will 
be required to use the technology to collaborate, conduct re- 
search, communicate and complete assignments. 
Teachers will guide student inquiry in conducting research, 
sharing data, resolving conflicts and problem solving. For this 
task they will need either experience in teaching with these new 
educational approaches, or specialized training or both. These 
educators will teach thematically, presenting a common core of 
learning 
as part of an interdisciplinary 
team, working in flexible 
and non-standard blocks of time, helping students create portfo- 
lios for assessment and be adept at using technology in the 
service 
of education. 
Once trained, even at this early grade, students will easily 
communicate over the telecommunication system with their 
distant peers on an almost daily basis, saving conversational 
interaction for those less frequent times when they can come 
together 
and work face-to-face. 
The same options will be true of their mentored relationships. 
The frequent teaching and guiding interactions permitted by 
student/mentor telecommunication will substantially accelerate 
the pace of project and inquiries. The mentors 
can also be 
contacted by E Mail which makes scheduling easier for the 
schools as well as the mentor. 
These same teaching patterns and configurations will be true at 
upper levels as well, with different and more demanding contex- 

— A-598 — 
tual requirements. Advanced applications will foster communi- 
cation and problem solving, including themes in social concerns 
such as human organization patterns, human development and 
environmental balances. 
At the end of the student’s high school junior year, it is antici- 
pated that they will have successfully completed enough re- 
quired credits to meet high school graduation guidelines. 
The last two high school years can position students so they are 
expected to spend an increasing portion of the class day at one of 
the metropolitan community colleges to begin additional studies 
at the college level, for which they will receive both high school 
and college credit. 
By the thirteenth year, the student will have achieved an Asso- 
ciate of Arts degree or the equivalent of two years completed of 
a four year college program. 
The character of the education we are Striving for will be 
oblivious to differences of ethnicity, gender, or cultural origin 
and highly sensitive to differences of academic skill and inquiry 
ability. 
Because the computing and multi-technology tools 
available today - the very tools that provide global networking 
and collaboration in the world of work - empower our students 
to become a part of this world, they will be prepared. They will 
be a generation of capable individuals who have continually 
grown in their ability to work productively and with satisfaction 
among peers who are unlike them in many ways. 
THE TRANSITIONAL PHASE 
This program will, by the third year, provide an unin 
stream for student progress from 6th grade through 13th grade 
and on to the Associate’s Degree. However, during years one 
and two, the program must begin with logical enclaves of pilot 
programs at selected grades.

Program Growth Chart 
In the first year, the program will be introduced at the SIXTH 
AND TWELFTH GRADE levels. To be accepted into the 
transitional “SHOW ME” program years, students will simply 
need to be academically on track (if in upper grades, require- 
ments fulfilled to graduate on time), and free of history of serious 
disciplinary problems. 
The Twelfth graders’ involvement in the transitional phase of 
“SHOW ME” will be natural. Facing the end of their public 
school career, they are likely to be very attracted by funding 
support for their first year of college. Unlike 
those who come 
after them, these students will have not grown adept in the 
“SHOW ME” program concepts and skills which five years of

will need readiness for graduation and ability to demonstrate

Transitional “SHOW ME” scholars will spend their mornings at 
the designated community college, guided by program faculty 
who accompany them from their high school and by matching 

— A-600 — 
program faculty at the community college. In the morning, they

bussed to the college campus and be return-bussed in the early

their usual way. 
In the third and final year, the program will begin to be an 
exclusive sequence, in which students may enter at 6th, 7th or 8th

grows smaller each year as it moves toward year thirteen, with 
“surviving” students having considerable commitment and abili-

Parent information and involvement will be an important part of 
this program since “SHOW ME’s” structure and grading will be 
new, and they will need to be at ease with the philosophy of the 
program and their child’s future in it. 
TEACHER STAFF DEVELOPMENT 
In the “SHOW ME” program, education will not happen in the 
traditional way, but in ways that correspond with future trends in 
education. 
To be effective, teachers will need both desire and flexibility to 
establish this new learning climate and these new social and 
intellectual growth patters between educator and student, stu- 
dent and student, or student and mentor. 
Inservice education, professional collaboration and experience 
will all be needed to establish teacher competence and comfort 
in: cooperative (core-knowledge) teaching, thematic content 
structuring and delivery of thematic content, flexible time com- 
mitments, professional collaboration for distance team teaching, 
itinerant teaching in several environments and portfolio assess- 
ment. 
— A-601 — 
Content mastery, beyond 
the teacher’s specialty discipline, will 
be required in: managing technology in education, conflict 
resolution, multi-cultural awareness and teaching inquiry groups. 
SUMMARY 
With “SHOW ME-Missouri”, students will be learning in a 
medium that reflects the quality of society today and eminently 
prepares them to evolve with society tomorrow, without gaps 
between the learning environment and the “real world”. 
This evolution 
capacity is in harmony with the premise of the 
January 1993 School Finance (Kinder) Court decision, Judge 
Kinder stating 
in his ruling to the Missouri state [sic] General 
Assembly that since education is a fundamental right, “it must be 
maintained at an ever-evolving level to assure the preservation 
of the rights and liberties of the people.” (pg. 28) 
The character of the education we are striving for will be 
oblivious 
to differences of ethnic, gender, 
or cultural origin and 
highly sensitive to differences of academic skill and inquiry 
ability. 
Because the computing and multi-technology tools 
available today - the very tools that provide global networking 
and collaboration in the world of work - empower our students 
to become a part of this world, they will be prepared. They will 
be a generation of capable individuals who have continually 
grown in their ability to work productively and with satisfaction 
among peers who are unlike them in many ways.
