Educating 
for a Peaceful World 
Morton Deutsch 
This article outlines a program of what schools can do to 
encourage the values, attitudes, and knowledge that foster 
constructive rather than destructive relations, which prepare 
children to live in a peaceful world. 
It describes four key 
components of such programs: cooperative learning, conflict 
resolution training, the constructive use of controversy in teach- 
ing subject matter, and the creation of dispute resolution centers 
in schools. 
Lewis P. Lipsitt served as action editor for this article. 
This article was presented 
as the presidential address to 
the division of Peace Psychology at the 99th Annual Con- 
vention of the American Psychological Association in San 
Francisco on August 3, 1991. 
Correspondence concerning this article should be ad- 
dressed tu Morton Deutsch, International Center for Coop- 
eration and Conflict Resolution, Teachers College, Colum- 
bia University, New York, NY 10027. 
Families and schools are the two most important institutions 
that influence developing children’s predispositions to hate and 
to love. Although the influence of the family comes earlier and 
is often more profound, there is good reason to believe that 
children’s subsequent experiences in schools can modify or 
strengthen their earlier acquired dispositions. In this article, I 
outline a program that schools can follow to encourage the 
development of the values, attitudes, and knowledge that foster 
constructive rather than destructive relations, which prepare 
children to live in a peaceful world. 
Many schools do not provide much constructive social expe- 
rience 
for students. Too often, schools are structured in ways that 

— A-604 — 
pit students against one another. They compete for teachers’ 
attention, for grades, for status, and for admission to prestigious 
schools. Being put down and putting down others are pervasive 
occurrences. Many of us can recall classroom experiences of 
hoping that another student, who was called on by the teacher 
instead of us, would give the wrong answer so that we could get 
called on and give the right answer. 
In recent years, it has been increasingly recognized that 
schools have to change in basic ways if we are to educate children 
so that they are for rather than against one another, so that they 
develop the ability to resolve their conflicts constructively rather 
than destructively and are prepared to live in a peaceful world. 
This recognition has been expressed in a number of interrelated 
movements: Cooperative learning, conflict resolution, and edu- 
cation for peace. In my view, there are four key components in 
these overlapping movements: cooperative learning, conflict 
resolution training, the constructive use of controversy in teach- 
ing subject matters, and the creation of dispute resolution centers 
in the schools.' I discuss each briefly, with more emphasis on 
cooperative learning and conflict resolution because | have 
worked more extensively in these two areas and because they 
provide a valuable base for education in constructive contro- 
versy and mediation. 
Cooperative Learning 
Although cooperative learning has many ancestors and can be 
traced back for at least 2,000 years, it is only in this century that 
there has been development of a theoretical base, systematic 
— A-605 — 
research, and systematic teaching procedures for cooperative 
learning. There are five key elements of cooperative learning 
(Johnson, Johnson, & Holubec, 1986). The most important is 
positive interdependence. Students must perceive that it is to 
their advantage if other students learn well and that it is to their 
disadvantage 
if others do poorly. This can be achieved 
in many 
different ways: through mutual goals (goal interdependence); 
division of labor (task interdependence); group members (re- 
source interdependence); and by giving joint rewards (reward 
interdependence). 
In addition, cooperative learning requires face-to-face inter- 
action in which students can express their positive interdepen- 
dence 
in behavior. It also requires individual accountability 
of 
each member of the cooperative learning group to one another 
for mastering the material to be learned and for providing 
appropriate support and assistance to each other. Furthermore, 
it is necessary for the students to be trained in the interpersonal 
and small group skills needed for effective cooperative work in 
groups. Finally, cooperative learning also involves providing 
students with the time and procedures for processing or analyz- 
ing how well their learning groups are functioning and what can 
be done to improve how they work together. It is desirable 
to 
compose cooperative learning groups that are heterogeneous 
with regard to gender, academic ability, ethnic background, and 
physical disability. 
Hundreds of research studies have been done on the relative 
effects of cooperative, competitive, and individualistic learning 
experiences (see Johnson 
& Johnson, 1983, 1989). The various 
studies of cooperative learning are quite consistent with one 
sceesahaleptiamdeoatmaeimetmeen totes tae 
effects of 
and 
ition (Deutsch, 1949a, 1 
in indicating 
ie tar Ricdite tines oc 
ceaaen Students de- 
velop a considerably greater commitment, helpfulness, and 
caring for each other regardless of differences in ability level, 

— A-606 — 
ethnic background, gender, social class or physical disability. 
They develop more skill in taking the perspective of others, 
emotionally as well as cognitively. They develop greater self- 
esteem and a greater sense of being valued by their classmates. 
They develop more positive attitudes toward learning, school, 
and their teachers. They usually learn more in the subjects they 
study by cooperative learning, and they acquire more of the skills 
and attitudes that are conducive to effective collaboration with 
others. 
It is evident that cooperative education fosters constructive 
relations. Moreover, when used by skillful teachers, it can help 
children overcome alienated or hostile orientations to others that 
they have developed from earlier experiences (see Johnson & 
Johnson, 1989, and Deutsch et al., 1992, for a more extensive 
discussion of mental health effects). 
However, it is important to realize that, although the concept 
of cooperative learning is simple, its practice is not. Changing 
a classroom and school so that they emphasize cooperative 
learning requires that teachers learn many new skills ways of 
teaching students cooperative skills, ways to monitor and inter- 
vene in student work groups to improve students’ collaborative 
skills, methods of composing student groups and structuring 
cooperative learning goals so that groups are likely to work well 
together, ways of developing curriculum materials to promote 
Positive interdependence, ways to create constructive academic 
controversies within the cooperative groups, and ways of inte- 
grating the cooperative learning with competitive and individu- 
alistic learning activities. It usually takes teachers about three to 
four years to become well skilled in the use of cooperative 
learning. 
— A-607 — 
Sometimes parents and teachers have misconceptions about 
cooperative learning that make them resistant to it initially.” 
There are several myths that it is well to confront (see Johnson 
et al., 1986, for a more extensive discussion). The following are 
four common myths. 
1. Cooperative learning does not prepare students for the adult 
world, which is highly competitive. There are two points to be 
made. (a) The ability of people to work cooperatively is crucial 
to building and maintaining stable marriages, families, commu- 
nities, friendships, careers, and a peaceful world. Although 
competition has often been stressed as the key to success in the 
world of work, the reality is that individual as well as corporate 
success depends on effective cooperation and teamwork (Kohn, 
1986). (b) Schools, even with extensive cooperative learning, 
would provide much experience with individual and group 
competition. 
The issue is not to eliminate competition and 
individualism 
from the schools but to provide a more appropriate 
balance with cooperation.’ Although children are exposed to 
much competition in schools, my impression is that schools 
* There has been little research on factors affecting the acceptance of or 
resistance 
to cooperative learning (conflict resolution, constructive contro- 
versy, or mediation) programs by teachers, parents, or school systems. My 
impression is that the interest and demand for such programs have been 
increasing at an accelerating rate during the past 10 years and that the supply 
of well-trained experts to train teachers and administrators in these areas is 
insufficient 
to meet the demand. In the near future, I expect that schools of 
education will develop educational programs for new teachers and adminis- 
trators 
in these areas. 
* Not enough research has been done yet to specify an appropriate balance 
among the different modes of teaching. It would undoubtedly 
vary as a 
function of such factors as the skills of the individual teacher, the type of 
subject matter to be learned, and the characteristics of the students. Never- 
theless, the available research indicates that, in a wide variety of contexts and 
subject matter with diverse students, the use of cooperative learning is 
beneficial. 

— A-608 — 
rarely teach in a systematic way generalizable skills for being 
effective competitors. 
2. High-achieving students are penalized by working in 
heterogeneous cooperative learning groups. Research evidence 
clearly indicates that high-achieving students learn at least as 
much in cooperatively structured classrooms as they do in the 
more traditional ones (Johnson & Johnson, 1983, 1989). They 
frequently learn more: Teaching less able students often solidi- 
fies their own laboratively, and they learn how to be mutually 
respecting despite differences in ability. This is not to deny that 
some high achievers and help from their teachers and their 
classmates to appreciate the benefits they can obtain from 
cooperative learning. It should also be recognized that coopera- 
tive learning does not imply that high achievers must learn and 
work at the same place as low achievers. Nor does it imply that 
high achievers will lack opportunities to work alone or to work 
cooperatively with other high achievers. 
3. Grading is unfair in cooperative learning. There are many 
ways of creating positive interdependence in cooperative learn- 
ing groups; group grading is one way but it is not necessary. 
When group grades are used, individual grades may also be used. 
Although students sometimes complain about grades, com- 
plaints appear to be less frequent in cooperative learning class- 
rooms than in more traditional ones. 
Students are able to 
recognize that how well people do in life is affected not only by 
how well they perform as individuals, but also by how well the 
groups, teams, corporations, and nations of which they are 
members perform. 
4. The good students do all the work, the lazy students get a 
free ride. A central feature in cooperative learning is individual 
accountability. 
If a student is “goofing off,” this becomes a 
problem for the group that, with encouragement and appropriate 
help from the teacher, the group can usually solve. In solving the 
problem, the group learns a great deal and the poorly motivated, 
— A-609 — 
alienated, withdrawn, or reclusive student often benefits enor- 
mously as he or she becomes an active participant in cooperative 
learning. 
Conflict Resolution Training 
Conflict is an inevitable feature of all social relations. conflict 
can take a constructive 
or destructive course, it can take 
the form 
of enlivening controversy or deadly quarrel. There is much to 
suggest that there is a two-way relation between effective coop- 
eration and constructive conflict resolution. Good cooperative 
relations facilitate the constructive management of conflict; the 
ability to handle constructively the inevitable conflicts that occur 
during cooperation facilitates the survival and deepening of 
cooperative relations. 
In recent years, conflict resolution training programs have 
sprouted in a number of schools, industries, and community 
dispute-resolution centers. 
In this article, I focus on such 
programs 
in schools. Although I believe these programs are very 
promising, they are relatively new and little systematic research 
on their effectiveness has been done. There are many different 
programs, and their contents vary with the age and background 
of the students. 
Nevertheless, there are some common elements running 
through most programs. They derive from the recognition that 
a constructive process of conflict resolution is similar to an 
effective, cooperative problem-solving process (in which the 
conflict is perceived as the mutual problem to be solved) whereas 
a destructive process is similar to a win-lose, competitive struggle 
(Deutsch, 1973). In effect, most conflict resolution training 
programs seek to instill attitudes, knowledge, and skills that are 
conducive to effective, cooperative problem solving and to 
discourage the attitudes and habitual responses that give rise to 
win-lose struggles. Below, I list the central elements included in 
many training programs, but I do not have the space to describe 

— A-610 — 
the ingenious techniques that are used in teaching them. The 
sequence in which they are taught varies as a function of the 
nature of the group being taught. 
1. Know what type of conflict you are involved in. There are 
three major types of conflict: the zero-sum conflict (a pure win- 
lose conflict), the mixed-motive (both can win, both can lose, or 
one can win and the other lose), and the pure cooperative (both 
can win or both can lose). It is important to know what kind of 
conflict you are in because the different types require different 
types of strategies and tactics (see Lewicki & Litterer, 1985; 
Pruitt & Rubin, 1986; Walton & McKersie, 1965). The common 
tendency is for inexperienced Parties to define their conflicts as 
win-lose even though it is a mixed-motive conflict. Very few 
conflicts are intrinsically win-lose conflicts, but if they are 
musperceived to be such, the parties involved are apt to engage 
in acompetitive, destructive process of conflict resolution. This 
is so unless there are very strong accepted norms or rules 
regulating the nature of the competitive interaction (as in com- 
petitive games). 
The strategies and tactics of the different types of conflict 
differ. In a zero-sum conflict one seeks to amass, mobilize, and 
use the various resources of power (Lasswell & Kaplan, 1950) in 
such a way that one can bring to bear on the conflict more 
effective, relevant power than one’s adversary. If this is not 
possible in the initial area of conflict, one seeks to transform the 
arena of conflict into one in which one’s effective power is 
greater than one’s adversary’s. Thus, if a bully challenges you 
toa fight because you won’t “lend” him or her money and he or 
she is 
stronger than you (and you cannot amass the power to 
deter, intimidate, or beat the bully), you might arrange to change 
ing the Police or other legal authority. 
Other Strategies and 
tactics in win-lose conflicts involve outwitting, misleading, 
— A-6li ~ 
seducing, blackmailing, and the various forms of the black arts 
that have been discussed by Machiavelli (1513/1950), Potter 
(1965), Schelling (1960), and Alinsky (1971), among others. 
The strategy and tactics involved in mixed-motive conflicts are 
discussed below. My emphasis 
is on the strategy of cooperative 
problem solving to find a mutually satisfactory solution to the 
conflict and on the development and application of mutually 
acceptable fair principles to handle situations in which the 
aspirations of both sides cannot be realized equally. The strategy 
and tactics of the resolution of cooperative conflicts involve 
primarily cooperative fact-finding and research as well as ratio- 
nal persuasion. 
2. Become aware of the causes and consequences of violence 
and of the alternatives to violence, even when you are very angry. 
Become realistically aware of how much violence there is, how 
many young people die from violence, the role of weapons in 
leading to violence, how frequently homicides are precipitated 
by arguments, and how alcohol and drugs contribute 
to violence. 
Become aware of what makes you very angry; learn the healthy 
and unhealthy ways you have to expressing anger. Learn how to 
actively channel your anger in ways that are not violent and are 
not likely 
to provoke violence 
from the other. Understand that 
violence begets violence and that if you “win” an argument by 
violence, the other will try to get even in some other way. Learn 
alternatives to violence in dealing with conflict. Prothrow- 
Smith (1987) has developed a very helpful curriculum for 
adolescents on the prevention of violence. 
3. Face conflict rather than avoid it. Recognize 
that conflict 
may make you anxious and that you may try to avoid it. Learn 
the typical defenses that you use to evade conflict (e.g., denial, 
suppression, becoming overly agreeable, rationalization, post- 
ponement, premature conflict resolution). Become 
aware of the 
negative consequences of evading a conflict, such as irritability, 
tension, and persistence 
of the problem. Learn 
what kinds of 

— A-612 — 
conflict are best avoided rather than confronted for example, 
conflicts that will evaporate shortly, those that are inherently 
unresolvable, and win-lose conflicts that you are unlikely to win. 
4. Respect yourself and your interests, and respect the other 
and his or her interests. 
Personal insecurity and sense of 
vulnerability often lead people to define conflicts as life or death, 
win-lose struggles even when they are relatively minor, mixed- 
inhibits the use of competitive tactics of power, coercion, depre- 
cation, and deception that commonly escalate the issues and 
often lead to violence. 
— A-613 — 
conversely, what they take as self-evident and right may not 
seem that way to you. Learn to understand and accept the reality 
of cultural differences; try to understand the other’s culture and 
try to help the other to understand yours. Expect cultural 
misunderstandings, and use them as opportunities for learning 
rather than as a basis of estrangement. 
6. Distinguish clearly between interests and positions. Posi- 
tions may be opposed, but interests may not be (Fisher & Ury, 
1981). The classic example from Follett (1940) is that of a 
brother and sister, each of whom wanted the only orange avail- 
able. The sister wanted the peel of the orange to make marma- 
lade; the brother wanted to eat the inner part. Their positions (“I 
want the orange”) were opposed, but their interests were not. 
Often when conflicting parties reveal their underlying interests, 
it is possible to find a solution that suits them both. 
7. Explore your interests and the other’ s interests to identify 
the common and compatible interests that you share. Identifying 
shared interests makes it easier to deal constructively with the 
interests that you perceive as being opposed. A full exploration 
of one another’s interests increases empathy and facilitates 
subsequent problem solving. For an excellent discussion of how 
to develop empathy and 
a 
sense of shared interests, see Schulman 
and Mekler (1985). 
When considerable distrust and hostility have developed 
between the conflicting parties, it may be useful to have third 
parties help in this process of exploration. The third parties may 
serve one or more functions. They may serve as facilitators 
or as 
conciliators (or therapists) who help the parties control and 
reduce their distrust and hostility enough to permit them to 
engage in this process themselves. They may serve as mediators 
who directly assist the parties in this process themselves. They 
may serve as mediators who directly assist the parties in this 
process or even undertake the exploration for the conflicting 
parties, doing what the parties are unable or unwilling to do. 

— A-614 — 
There has been considerable discussion of such third-party 
intervention (including the selection, training, and ethical re- 
quirements for third parties) in Folberg and Taylor (1984), 
Kelman (1979), Kressel (1985), Kressel, Pruitt, and Associates 
(1989), and Rubin (1980). 
8. Define the conflicting interests between yourself and the 
other as a mutual problem to be solved cooperatively. Define the 
conflict in the smallest terms possible, as a “here-now-this” 
conflict rather than as a conflict between personalities or general 
principles that is, as a conflict about a specific behavior rather 
than about who is a better person. Diagnose the problem Clearly, 
and then seek creative new options that lead to mutual gain. If 
no option for mutual gain can be discovered, seek to agree on a 
fair rule or procedure for deciding how the conflict will be 
resolved. However, not all conflicts can be solved to mutual 
Satisfaction even with the most creative thinking. In such cases, 
agreeing on a fair procedure to determine who gets his or her 
way, or seeking help from neutral third parties when such an 
agreement cannot be reached, may be the most constructive 
resolution possible (see Lewicki & Litterer, 1985, for an excel- 
lent discussion of the strategy and tactics of integrative bargain- 
ing). To the extent that the parties see the possibility of a 
mutually satisfying agreement, they will be more able to listen to 
one another in an understanding, empathic manner, and, of 
course, the converse is true too. 
9. In communicating with the other, listen attentively and 
speak so as to be understood. This requires an active effort to 
take the perspective of the other and to check continually your 
success in doing so. You should listen to the other’s meaning and 
emotion in such a way that the other feels understood as well as 
is understood. Similarly, you want to communicate to the other 
your thoughts and feelings in such a way that you have good 
evidence that he or she understands the way you think and feel. 
— A-615 — 
The feeling of being understood, as well as effective communi- 
cation, facilitates constructive resolution. 
Johnson and Johnson (1987), Lewicki and Litterer (1985), 
Prutzman, Stern, Burger, and Bodenhamer (1988), and many 
others have provided excellent discussions and practical exer- 
cises for developing effective communicating and listening 
skills. As a communicator, you want to be skilled in obtaining 
and holding the other’s attention, in phrasing your communica- 
tion so that it is readily comprehended and remembered, and in 
acquiring the credibility that facilitates acceptance of your 
message. Skills in taking others’ perspectives 
and obtaining 
feedback about the effectiveness of your communications are 
important. Listening actively and effectively entails not only 
taking the perspective of the other so that you understand the 
communicator’ s ideas and feelings but also communicating your 
desire to understand the other and indicating, through paraphras- 
ing your understanding 
or through questions, what you do not 
understand. Role reversal seems to be helpful in developing an 
understanding of the other’s perspective and providing checks 
on how effective the communication 
process has been. 
perception and thought interfere with communication, 
make 
empathy difficult, and impair problem solving. Psychologists 
can provide a checklist of the common forms of 
mi 
i 
include black-white thinking, demonizing the other, shortening 
your time perspective, narrowing your range of perceived op- 
tions, and the fundamental 
attribution error. The fundamental 
attribution error is illustrated in the tendency to attribute the 
aggressive actions of the other to the other’s personality while 
attributing your own aggressive actions to external circum- 
stances (such as the other’s hostile actions). The ability to 

— A-616 — 
recognize and admit your misperceptions and misjudgments 
Clears the air and facilitates similar acknowledgment by the other 
(see Jervis, 1976; Kahnemen, Slovic, & Tversky, 1982; Nisbett 
& Ross, 1980). 
€xcept 
on his or her terms, or to someone who plays dirty tricks 
(deceives, welshes 
on an agreement, personally attacks you, etc.) 
if you realize that you usually have a choice: You don’t have to 
Stay in the relationship with the other. You are more likely to be 
aware of your freedom to choose between leaving or staying if 
you feel that there are alternatives to continuing the relationship 
that you can accept. The alternative may not be great, but it may 
be better than staying in the relationship. The freedom 
to choose 
prevents the other, if he or she benefits from the relationship, 
from making the relationship unacceptable to you. 
Second, it is useful to be open and explicit to the other about 
what he or she is doing that is upsetting you and to indicate the 
effects that these actions are having on you. If the other asserts 
that you have misunderstood or denies doing what you have 
stated, and if you are not persuaded, be 
forthright in 
maintaining 
that this remains a problem for you. Discuss with the other what 
could be done to remove the problem (your misunderstanding of 
the other, your need for reassurance, or the other’s noxious 
— A-617 — 
A phrase that I have found useful in characterizing the stance 
one should take in difficult (as well as easy) conflicts is to be 
“firm, fair, and friendly.” Firm in resisting intimidation, exploi- 
tation, and dirty tricks; fair in holding to one’s moral principles 
and not reciprocating the other’s immoral behavior despite his or 
her provocations; and in the sense that one is willing to initiate 
and reciprocate cooperation. 
12. Know yourself, and how you typically respond in differ- 
ent sorts of conflict situations. As I have Suggested earlier, 
conflict frequently evokes anxiety. In clinical work, I have 
found that the anxiety is often based on unconscious fantasies of 
being overwhelmed and helpless in the face of the other's 
aggression or of being so angry and aggressive that you will 
destroy the other. Different people deal with their anxieties 
about conflict in different ways. I have found it useful to 
emphasize six different dimensions of dealing with conflict that 
can be used to 
characterize a person’ s predispositions to respond 
(a) Avoiding conflict/excessively involved in conflict. Con- 
flict avoidance is expressed in denial, repression, suppression, 
avoidance, and continuing postponement of facing the conflict. 
Sometimes it is evidenced in premature conflict resolution, 

— A-618 — 
fleeing into an agreement before there is adequate exploration of 
the conflicting interests and the various options for resolving the 
conflict. Usually, the conflict that is avoided does not go away, 
rather, the tension associated with it is expressed in fatigue, 
irritability, muscular tension, and a sense of malaise. Excessive 
involvement in conflict is sometimes expressed in a “macho” 
attitude, a chip 
on one’s shoulder, a tendency to seek out conflict 
to demonstrate that one is not afraid of conflict. It is also 
commonly expressed in a preoccupation with conflict -obsessive 
thoughts about fights, disputes, and quarrels, with much rehears- 
ing of moves and counter moves between oneself and one’s 
adversaries. Presumably, a healthy predisposition involves the 
readiness to confront conflict when it arises without needing to 
seek it out or to be preoccupied with it. 
(b) Hard/soft. 
Some people are prone to take a tough, 
aggressive, dominating, unyielding response to conflict, fearing 
that otherwise they will be taken advantage of and be considered 
soft. Others are afraid that they will be considered mean, hostile, 
or presumptuous, and as a consequence, they are excessively 
gentle and unassertive. They often expect the other to read their 
minds and know what they want even though they are not open 
in expressing their interests. A more appropriate stance is a firm 
support of one’s own interests combined with a ready respon- 
siveness to the interests of the other. 
(c) RigidNoose. Some people immediately seek to organize 
and control the situation by setting the agenda and defining the 
rules. They feel anxious if things threaten to get out of control 
and feel threatened 
by the unexpected. As a consequence, 
they 
are apt to push for rigid arrangements and rules and get upset by 
even minor deviations. At the other extreme, there are some 
people who are aversive to anything that seems formal, limiting, 
conditional, or constricting. They prefer a loose, improvisa- 
tional, informal arrangement in which rules and procedures are 
implicit rather than overt. An approach that allows for both 
— A-619 — 
orderliness and flexibility in dealing with the conflict seems 
more constructive than one that is compulsive either in its 
organizing or in its rejection of orderliness. 
(d) 
JIntellectual/emotional. 
At one extreme, emotion is 
repressed, controlled, or isolated so that no relevant emotion is 
felt or expressed as one communicates one’s thoughts. The 
appearance is of someone who is calm, rational, and detached. 
Frequently, beneath the calm surface is the fear that if one feels 
or expresses one’s emotions, they will get out of control and one 
will do something destructive, foolish, or humiliating. However, 
the lack of appropriate emotional expressiveness may seriously 
impair communication. The other may take your lack of emotion 
as an indicator that you have no real commitment to your 
interests and that you lack genuine concern for the other’s 
interests. At the other extreme, there are some people who 
believe that only feelings are real and that words and ideas are not. __ 
to be taken seriously unless they are thoroughly soaked in 
emotion. The emotional intensity of such people also interferes 
with communication. 
It impairs the ability to explore ideas 
mutually and to develop creative solutions to impasses; it also 
makes it difficult to differentiate the significant from the insig- 
nificant, if even the trivial is accompanied with intense emotion. 
The ideal mode of communication combines thought and affect: 
The thought is supported by the affect, and the affect is explained 
by the thought. 
(e) Escalating/minimizing. At one extreme, there are people 
who tend to experience any given conflict in the largest possible 
terms. The issues are cast so that what is at stake involves one’s 
self, one’s family, one’s ethnic group, precedence for all time, or 
the like. The specifics of the conflict get lost as it escalates along 
the various dimensions of conflict: the size and number of the 
immediate issues involved; the number of motives and partici- 
pants implicated on each side of the issue; the size and number 
of the principles and precedents that are perceived to be at stake; 

— A-620 — 
the cost that the participants are willing to bear in relation to the 
conflict; the number of norms of moral conduct from which 
behavior toward the other side is exempted; and the intensity 
of 
negative attitudes toward the other side. 
Escalation ae 
tively except when the escalation proceeds so rapidly that its 
—_—~ 
behavior toward the other that one would otherwise consider 
morally reprehensible. 
Such behavior escalates conflict and 
turns it in the direction of violence and destruction. 
The foregoing elements could provide the basis for many 
different types of courses and workshops in conflict resolution in 
schools. My limited eaperience 
with such training suggests that, 
by itself, a simple course or workshop 
is not usually sufficient 
to 
produce lasting effects. Students must have repeated opportuni- 
ties to practice their skills of constructive conflict resolution in 
a supportive atmosphere. The use of constructive controversy in 
teaching could provide such an atmosphere. 
The Use of Constructive Controversy in Teaching Subject 
Matters 
David Johnson and Roger Johnson (1987, 1992) of the Univer- 
sity of Minnesota have suggested that teachers, no matter what 
subjects they teach, can stimulate and structure constructive 
controversy in the classroom that will promote academic learn- 
ing and the development of conflict resolution skills. A coopera- 
tive context is established for a controversy, for example, by 
assigning students to groups of four, dividing each group into 
two pairs who are assigned positions on the topics to be dis- 
cussed, and requiring each group to reach a consensus on the 
issue and turn in a group report on which all members will be 
evaluated. 
There are five phases involved in the structured 
controversy. 
First, the paired students learn their respective 
positions: then each pair presents its position. Next, there is an 
open discussion in which students argue strongly and persua- 
sively for their positions. 
After this, there is a perspective 
reversal, in which each pair presents the opposing pair’s position 
as sincerely and persuasively 
as it can. In the last phase, 
they 
drop their advocacy of their assigned positions and seek to reach 
consensus on a position that is supported by the evidence. In this 
phase, they write a joint statement with the rationale and support- 
ing evidence for the synthesis their group has agreed on. 

— A-622 — 
The discussion rules that the students are instructed to follow- 
ing during the controversy are (a) be critical of ideas, not people; 
(b) focus on making the best possible decision, not on winning; 
(c) encourage everyone to participate; (d) listen to everyone’s 
ideas, even if you do not agree; (e) restate what someone has said 
if it is not clear; (f) bring out the ideas and facts supporting both 
sides and then try to put them together in a way that makes sense; 
(g) try to understand both sides of the issue; and (h) change your 
mind if the evidence clearly indicates that you should do so. 
After the structured controversy, there is group processing 
and highlighting of the specific skills required for constructive 
controversy. There is good reason to believe that such structured 
controversy not only would make the classroom more interesting 
but would also promote the development of perspective taking, 
critical thinking, and other skills involved in constructive con- 
flict resolution. However, as yet there has been little systematic 
research on structured controversy. 
Mediation 
in the Schools 
There are difficult conflicts that the disputing parties may not 
be able to resolve constructively without the help of third parties 
acting as mediators. In schools, such conflicts can occur be- 
tween students, between students and teachers, between parents 
and teachers, and between teachers and administrators. To deal 
with such conflicts, mediation programs have been established 
in a number 
of schools. These programs vary, but typically 
students and teachers are given about 20 to 30 hours of training 
in the principles of constructive conflict resolution and specific 
training in how to serve |.s mediators. They are usually 
given a 
set of rules to apply during the mediation process. Students 
as 
young 
as 10 years old as well as high school and college students 
have been trained. Little systematic research on the effects of 
such programs has been done, but there is considerable anecdotal 
evidence to suggest that many student mediators have benefited 
— A-623 — 
enormously and that incidents of school violence have de- 
creased. 
In selecting to emphasize cooperative learning, conflict reso- 
lution, structured controversy, and school mediation as the core 
of any comprehensive program for a peaceful world, I have been 
guided by the view that students need to have continuing expe- 
riences of constructive conflict resolution as they learn different 
subjects, as well as an immersion in a school environment that 
provides daily experiences (and a model) of cooperative rela- 
tions and of constructive resolution of conflicts. This pervasive 
and extended experience, combined with tuition in the concepts 
and principles of cooperative work and of conflict resolution, 
should enable the students to develop generalizable attitudes and 
skills strong enough to resist the countervailing influences that 
are so prevalent in their nonschool environments. It is my hope 
that, by the time they become adults, they would have developed 
the attitudes, knowledge, and skills that would enable them to 
cooperate with others in resolving constructively the inevitable 
conflicts that will occur among and within nations, ethnic 
groups, communities, and families.

VOLUNTARY STUDENT TRANSFER 
IN 
METROPOLITAN KANSAS CITY 
Cross-District Research and Discovery: 
Sample Units and Projected Units 
Multi-District Discovery Units 
“Cooperative learning” is a crucial feature of the Voluntary 
Student Transfer project, a feature made possible as both dis- 
tance learning and face-to-face learning by the universal use of 
electronic technology. Fundamental to it will be a new kind of 
educational literature and teaching approach: “Cross-District 
Research and Discovery Units.” 
These teaching/learning tools are like an old-style curriculum, 
providing structure and strategy for learning to unfold. They are 
also unique. The learning they facilitate will unite young scholar 
communities, across distances, in common inquiry, reflection 
and conclusion. Because this plan supports intermittant [sic] 
meetings in non-threatening environments with mentors and 
distant teammates, this way of learning will have prepared them 
to work seamlessly together on those occasions. 
Section 1: Cross-District Discovery Unit Examples 
To make full use of so many available electronic resources, and 
to support learner-centered education, these open-ended materi- 
als are organized around themes. In your preview material is a 
complete discovery unit sample, “A Dream of a Team”, which, 
though extensive, only develops one part of the numerous 
possibilities of the theme TEAM. 
In this case, TEAM is presented as a science-emphasis cl uster 
around hypothesis testing, with features of computation, poetry, 
history, geography and current events. There could easily be 
literature and arts applications, as well as many others, all this 
— A-627 — 
with the TEAM concept narrowly defined as “sports” and even 
more narrowly defined as “baseball.” What are the possibilities 
for learning if the TEAM is from NASA, the TEAM is a family 
or the TEAM is a government 
entity? Thematic learning is 
limitless, layered for topic, depth and complexity. This is the 
reason it suits the new education so well. 
A second example appears in your review materials from the 
Olin Environmental Project. Here, a manufacturer has funded 
creation of cross-district explorations by a cadre of teachers 
under the guidance 
of PEI, using Olin chemical engineers as 
mentor personnel. Already completed, with more being added, 
this shows a different application of discovery unit learning 
around the theme WATER. 
Section 2: Projected Cross-District Discovery Units 
Several projects are currently contributing new Cross District 
Discovery Units to the ShareNet “stable.” Examples of current 
projects making contributions similar to the Olin materials 
include: The National Fish and Wildlife Agency project, the 
Jackson County Anti-Drug Tax project (developing prevention 
materials for access by the twelve county school districts and all 
other system users, which are ready to be incorporated 
into the 
“discovery format”), and the Oncology project with Indepen- 
dence Regional Health Center, providing cancer prevention 
lessons which can also be easily incorporated into discovery. 
Further, PEI staff continually generate new theme possibilities, 
then seek a funding “home” for them so that they can become 
available for use in projects such as Voluntary Student Transfer. 
This section lists a few of those incipient cross-district discovery 
units.

Cultural Diversity 
BALANCING UNIT AND DIVERSITY 
Our study of 
cultural diversity begins with ourselves and our 
families. Some of us may feel that “we” are just plain Americans 
and that we have no distinct heritage. This suit explores 
how ali 
of us have been influenced by beliefs, customs, and expectations 
which come from national, racial, or religious groups. Cuiture 
and ethnicity influence our language, food, society, and family 
relationships. Even those of us who do not ider‘ify with an 
ethnic background, are part of the American heritage that has 
been shaped and influenced by Native Americans and immi- 
grants 
from diverse cultures. 
Study Questions: 
What is meant by “unity” with respect to humanity? What is 
meant by “diversity” with respect to humanity? 
What factors tend to separate humans into different groups and 
therefore reveal our diversity? 
What factors tend to bring humans together for common pur- 
poses 
and therefore reveal 
our unity? 
What are some possible advantages of cultural and physical 
diversity 
in human populations? What are some possible 
disad- 
vantages 
or dangers? 
How have immigration patterns and laws affected the changing 
population 
mix in the United States? 
Discuss the prime example of forced immigration in the United 
States and how it has affected our history and development?

Literature Review 
Classes of students will begin with a short story unit using a story 
called “correspondence”. Pen pals is the central theme of the 
story. This will help students to understand the concept, provide 
and stimulate ideas for them to begin the “sharing process” 
electronically. Their first “pen pal” exercise will be to create 
personal profiles and sent to their respective pals. These will be 
followed by reading and exchanges on the following works, The 
Pigman; The Odyssey; Of Mice and Men; Romeo and Juliet.

— A-635 — 
Welcome to Share Net’s Cross District discovery unit, 
“A Dream of a Team” 
which is designed to be used by a teacher who has no experi- 
ence with teaching statistics as well as the statistical veteran. 
Using data.on approximately 150 baseball cards supplied to 
you 
by Pan Ed, your students are going to buy a team, make 
hypotheses, gather data within their own groups and class, 
assimilate, graph, and analyze the data, and use the analysis to 
evaluate their hypotheses. 
Then, via Share Net, they will share their findings and data 
with the other schools involved in the project, compare their own 
conclusions with those made by students in the other schools, 
assimilate and organize the large collection of data and use it to 
analyze their hypotheses again. 
Your students will have the opportunity 
to “buy” players from 
other schools in an attempt to build a Dream Team - | 1 positions, 
and track the financial activities that are involved. The most 
complete team(s) will receive a prize on the last day of the 
exercise. All students will be rewarded for their participation 
with a special gift the last day of the exercise. 
Your students will be computing, communicating both orally 
and in writing, be involved with technology through telecommu- 
nications, and have the opportunity to relate to the world at large 
by buying a team and by contacting mentors. 
Please remember that all questions in the discussions are 
intended 
to be guidelines only. Let your creativity 
reign. Be sure 
to put all of your related Data on the system for comparative 
analysis. 

— A-636 — 
Thanks for participating, and may your baseball team buying, 
Statistics, and telecommunications experience be a memorable 
one for you and your students. 
Wishing you success, 
Pan Ed and Share Net 
Preparation: 
In December, sign up to be a part of the Cross District 
Discovery Unit through Share Net. 
Download the lessons you need to prepare yourself 
and/or your students in basic statistical methods. 
Incorporate into your daily plans enough time to 
prepare your students with Share Net statistical activi- 
ties or other activities of your choice. 
Download all lessons for the unit and, if needed, other 
Statistics lessons listed in BACKGROUND.

Time Frame — You will need: 
* 
Approximately 1 week previous to the Cross 
District Activities to prepare students for basis 
statistical graphing and analysis methods if they 
have had little or no experience with that kind of 
mathematics. 
* 
5 -6weeks (2-3 sessions per week) to accomplish 
the Cross District sharing data activities.

graphing and analysis. If your students do need basic work, 
begin this introduction 5 - 6 class days before starting Block 1.

c. 
Then what percent will each of the bars on our

those? (Ans. each bar height divided by the total

a percent.)

— A-672 — 
Olin Environmental Project 
This section includes hard copy of materials in the current water 
treatment interactive learning package: 
1. “StarDate 2210 *...And Not a Drop to Drink’” 
This is an interactive narrative built on the Star Trek theme 
familiar to most children of this age. It is the teaching framework 
which presents subject content to the student and positions the 
various Classroom experiences to act as illustrations. 
Because of the benefits of electronic presentation, the student 
may read the teaching story from the screen or print it as hard 
copy; may respond to text questions and then check answers filed 
at the “Ask Captain Olin” feature; may generate their own 
questions and expect an answer from Olin personnel within 
twenty four hours; or may take on the final project with an 
assigned Olin staff member providing distant mentoring through 
the telecommunication system. 
Note: If the project continues, lessons in each of the other three 
areas will be developed around subsequent installments of the 
“StarDate 2220” Interactive Narrative. 
2. 
Narrative Integrated Lessons 
Lesson 1: 
“The Case of The Wasteful Water Fountain” 
Lesson 2: 
“Dirty Water Comes Clean” 
Lesson 3: 
“Contamination: 
It CAN Be One In A Mil- 
lion” and, 
Lesson 4: 
“Safe Water: What You See is NOT What 
You Get.” 
3. 
Teacher Plan for Water Analysis Spring Project 
— A-673 — 
Interactive Narrative: StarDate 2220 
“,.. And Not A Drop To Drink” 
Water 
These are the voyages of the Starship Destiny. Our mission is 
to seek out truth, protect planet earth and boldly go where few 
have gone before, but ALL must 
now follow . 
. . 
Ship’s Crew 
Captain - Ben J. Olin 
First Officer - Lisa Waters 
Science Officer - Tom Silver 
Environmental Officer - John Green 
Navigator - Nancy Knight 
Engineer - Lee Sun 
Ship’s Doctor - Aleeta Crystal 
Captain’s Log: 
Our STARSHIP DESTINY has been damaged in a meteor 
shower. We are on impulse power, and most of our survival 
equipment 
has been destroyed. Sensors have found 
a deserted 
planet that can be reached on impulse power. 
I, Captain Olin, plan to take a landing party to the Planet Natura. 
Question: What 5 things should the landing crew look for to help 
them decide whether life can exist on this planet? (Compare your 
answers to this and all StarDate questions to answers in ShareNet 
at Curriculum, Science 4-6, Reference, “Ask Captain Olin”) 
After visiting Natura and examining records there, we have 
determined that the planet was once occupied by a 
civilization 

— A-674 — 
called Ecoes. The Ecoes, living in harmony with nature, had 
been on the planet for millions of years. They were invaded by 
aliens call Pollutos, who used up all the resources on the planet 
and polluted it so severely that both the original inhabitants and 
the invaders perished. 
Natura’s pollution happened hundreds of years ago and the 
planet has been healing itself since then. We find that the 
atmosphere on the planet has recovered and there is plenty of 
oxygen for human life forms to survive. The water, however, is 
a different matter. There are a few lakes with clean water, but this 
source is limited. 
The majority of the water on the planet 
continues to be polluted. 
Our first guess is that the natural rainfall on Natura, as on earth, 
is replenishing a portion of the water supply. 
Question: What things might the Pollutos have done to destroy 
the water on Natura? (Answers at “Ask Captain Olin’’) 
As we explore Natura, First Officer Lisa Waters questions 
whether we can survive here, but our crippled ship can not take 
us any further. 
We have found an old book that the Ecoes used to manage their 
world. Its knowledge agrees with ours, stating that one of the 
basic elements for life is water and that without water, life is 
impossible. In fact, as on earth, life began in water. The bodes 
of living things, the extinct Ecoes as well as humans, are made 
up mostly 
of water. Also, as on our home planet, the water on 
Natura is as old as the planet itself. Every molecule of water is 
recycled over and over. 
Natura has the same percentage of 
usable water as earth. 
Question: What percent of the water on earth is usable? (To 
compare your research answer, see “Ask Captain Olin’”’) 
John Green, Environmental Officer, has just come to me with a 
grave concern. The crew members are, of course, drinking and 
— A-675 — 
bathing in the available water. He estimates that this precious 
water supply will be exhausted within months if we cannot find 
a way to treat both the wastewater generated from our crew’ s use 
and the existing bodies of polluted water on the planet. But even 
before we address this task, we must determine whether any of 
the usage water is actually begin wasted by the crew. Lee Sun, 
Ship’s Chief Engineer, has examined the drinking fountain 
delivery system which the crew has rigged to siphon usable 
water from the mountain stream, and he has serious doubts about 
its efficiency. 
Chief Engineer Sun asks you help him determine whether the 
drinking fountain is wasting our precious water: 
Activity #1: 
Through direct observation, measure the amount of water 
actually drunk by humans using the fountain delivery 
system and how much water actually 
flows down the drain 
during the drinking time and compare them to judge the 
Amount wasted. (See Olin Environmental Project Lesson 
1, “The Case of the Wasteful Water Fountain”, on ShareNet 
at Curriculum, Science 4-6) 
Ihave asked Science Officer Tom Silver to explain to you and the 
other Senior Officers how water 
is naturally recycled 
so that we 
may understand what is needed to successfully treat polluted 
water. 
Commander Silver: 
“Like our home planet, Natura is NOT 
running 
out of water. The actual amount of water on both planets 
remains constant, but it is entirely possible to run out of US- 
ABLE water. 
First I wiJ) explain the water cycle, also known as the hydrologic 
(hy dro log’ ik) cycle. The hydrologic 
cycle is tremendously 
powerful, using more energy in only one day than humankind 
has generated throughout all of history on planet earth. 

— A-676 — 
At one time, only about .005 percent of the total water supply on 
earth is moving through the hydrologic cycle. A drop of water 
spends about nine days passing through the air; once it falls as 
precipitation (rainfall), it may remain in a glacier for 40 years. A 
water molecule may remain in the ocean for 40,000 years before 
being cycled, but eventually, every drop of water on Earth is 
moved through the hydrologic cycle and is used over and over 
and over again. 
“The water cycle on Natura, as well as Earth, starts from water 
vapor gathering in clouds. Wind cools the vapor, turning it into 
a liquid (rain) or a solid (snow, hail and sleet). Gravity makes the 
rain fall to the planet’s surface. Some water runs across the land 
into bodies of surface water - lakes, streams, oceans, etc. Other 
water soaks into the ground, pulled by gravity through layers of 
different materials in a process called “percolation” (per co la’ 
Shun). Percolated water is stored in the ground in “aquifers” 
(ah’kwa furz), which are layers of rock, gravel and sand. This 
water slowly moves through the aquifer toward the sea. Even- 
tually, ground water becomes surface water, too. For example, 
it may bubble up through the ocean floor, or appear on the land 
as a spring. The sun heats surface water, turning it into vapor that 
rises into the sky. And the cycle begins again! The sun is the 
“pump” 
that keeps water going around and around. 
The polluted water generated by your crew, as well as Natura’s 
existing polluted bodies of water, will eventually be cleaned by 
nature. But our crew cannot wait the potentially hundreds of 
years that would take. 
The Ecoe’s book explains the ancient art of wastewater treat- 
ment. Wastewater treatment speeds up the natural treatment 
process. We must learn this lost skill if our crew is going to 
survive. 
Before wastewater 
can be treated, it must be collected. The most 
efficient method is collection by gravity. 
Since Natura has 
a AGT? 
approximately the same gravity as earth, this collection method 
can be used. In cases where the land is too flat for gravity to move 
the water, or when the water must go above the land level, a pump 
can be used to transport the wastewater to the treatment area. 
Once the wastewater has been collected, the treatment begins. 
Wastewater treatment consists of physical, biological and chemi- 
cal processes. The wastewater is either removed by filtration 
(primarily treatment), which I will explain, or consumed by 
bacteria as its food source (secondary treatment), which will be 
described by Doctor Crystal. 
The first step in primary treatment will be removing large solids 
from the wastewater. This can be done by placing a metallic 
screen at the beginning of the wastewater treatment process to 
catch the large solids as wastewater passes through. These solids 
will have to be removed periodically to keep the filter from 
plugging. 
Because of damage 
to the ship’s computer 
by the meteor shower, 
we cannot run a diagnostic of this process the Ecoes described to 
determine its efficiency. Therefore, 
the captain has approved my 
request to assemble a team to physically test whether the basics 
of the Ecoe’s procedure for restoring dirty water actually works. 
You are included on that team...” 
Activity #2 
Demonstrate basic water treatment using dirty (muddy) 
water passing it through a funnel and producing clean 
water. (See Olin Environmental Project Lesson 2, “Dirty 
Water Comes Clean”, on ShareNet Curriculum, Science 4- 
6) 
“The second step in the treatment process is a grit chamber where 
heavy materials that might damage equipment or interfere with 
later processing are removed from the wastewater. The speed 
of 
the flow of the wastewater is decreased as wastewater moves 

— A-678 — 
through a sedimentation tank. Solids sink to the bottom of this 
tank in a process called ‘precipitation’ (pre-cip-i-ta’-shun) in 
which heavier particles fall out of a liquid to the bottom. They 
are then removed. 
This mass of solids is now called sludge 
(slu’j). Again, gravity is used to separate the grit from the 
wastewater. 
The third step is the settling tanks. In these tanks additional 
heavy solids will settle to the bottom, and are also removed as 
sludge. 
The Ecoes refer to the first three treatment process as ‘primary 
treatment’. In the first three treatment methods only gravity, a 
physical force, is used to separate various sized waste particles. 
As Medical Officer, Dr. Aleeta Crystal will explain the largely 
biological process of secondary treatment.” 
Dr. Crystal: “The next major treatment the Ecoes have recorded 
is ‘secondary treatment’, in which biological and/or chemical 
process are used to treat the wastewater. 
Biological treatment processes use bacteria to remove solids 
from the wastewater. The bacterium actually eats the organic 
solids, settles to the bottom of a tank, and the solids (which 
consist of the well fed bacteria and their food) are collected as 
sludge. The water remaining is clear and can be returned to a 
stream. On Natura as well as on Earth, bacteria are abundant. 
Bacteria are usually associated in our minds with diseases, 
however the Ecoes have found that on Natura, like Earth, only 
two percent of all the bacteria is harmful and all the rest perform 
useful or crucial functions, such as these “house cleaning” 
actions of eating things that would otherwise harm us. 
In order to use bacteria as a wastewater treatment method, we 
must provide the right mixture of oxygen and food. The Ecoes 
knew that bacteria are more efficient when oxygen is mixed at 
the ideal concentration so they can live, multiply and eat. 
— A-679 — 
The bacteria are key to the three major biological treatment 
process ; activated sludge, trickling filters and lagoons. 
Activated sludge is a treatment method where wastewater, 
microorganisms (bacteria), and oxygen are added together in a 
tank. Air is pumped into the wastewater in a process called 
“aeration” (air a’ shun). During aeration microorganisms absorb 
oxygen and eat the organic material. After a few hours, 
the 
wastewater is piped to another sedimentation tank to remove the 
remaining solids. The solids (which are the bacteria and all 
they’ 
ve eaten) settle to the bottom of the tank and are removed 
as Sludge. 
Trickling filters distribute the wastewater over a bed of small 
solid objects called ‘media’ which may be rocks, plastic, wood 
or aluminum cans. The solid media is approximately 0.4 to 1.8 
meters deep. As the wastewater flows over the solid media, the 
wastewater is aerated and the microorganisms form a biological 
growth layer in which they live while they consume most of the 
organic material in the wastewater. The growth layer (which 
consists of well-fed microorganisms and which is thick and easy 
to see) eventually falls off the solid media and is collected, again, 
as Sludge. A new growth layer will grow to replace it. 
In Lagoon wastewater treatment, pollutants are broken up through 
the use of various microorganisms. The lagoons 
are usually | to 
1.5 meters deep. This process uses sunlight, algae, microorgan- 
isms and oxygen to remove organic material 
and nutrients from 
the wastewater. There are two types of microorganisms 
used in 
lagoon treatment: 
aerobic (ai ‘ro bik) (microorganisms 
that use 
oxygen in the process of consuming the pollutants), and anaero- 
bic (an 
ai ‘ro bik) bacteria which eat different pollutants and 
release nitrogen and phosphorus which feed algae, in turn 
replacing oxygen used up by the aerobic bacteria. Because these 
have opposite effects on oxygen, they create a balanced action. 
The final treatment process is disinfection. This process kills 
disease-causing organisms by addition of a disinfectant chemi- 

— A-680 — 
cal such as chlorine. 
Ultraviolet light rays can also disinfect 
wastewater. 
The wastewater could now safely be discharged to local surface 
waters, and the Destiny crew’s water problems would be over.” 
Captain’s Log: 
Doctor Crystal has come to me with a report of numerous 
bacteria-caused illnesses developing among crew members sta- 
tioned in the field on this planet, but not among those who remain 
aboard the Destiny. It is her opinion that because the ship has no 
extra power to run their diagnostic tricorders when they are out 
on the planet’s surface, crewmembers are guessing at what water 
is safe to drink, basing their decision only on appearance. 
I directed our Medical Officer to call together all crew members 
and instruct them that very small amounts of some pollutants are 
still hazardous to their health, that judgements of water quality 
made through their senses are unreliable, and that they must 
bring all samples on board to the medical lab for analysis. 
I am including her instructions here. 
Activity #3 
Demonstrate simple water purity test consisting of 5 glasses 
of water filled with common household liquids such as weak 
tea, coffee, mixture of food coloring in water, water and 
suspended mud and add one or two drops of gasoline to 
water. Ask students which water samples appear to be 
pure. (See Olin Environmental Project Lesson 3, “Dirty 
Water Comes Clean”, on ShareNet at Curriculum, Science 
4-6.) 
Activity #4 
Demonstrate serial dilution using three glass beakers with 
different concentration of diluted food coloring. Making 
— A-681 — 
serial dilutions can help students grasp the concept of 
minute concentrations of pollutants. By beginning with a 
known volume of a pollutant (one milliliter), and using a 
factor of ten for each dilution step, a concentration of parts 
permilioncan be completed in three steps. The student can 
determine if color 
is still visible at one part per million. (See 
Olin Environmental Project Lesson 4, “Safe Water: What 
You See Is NOT What You Get!” , on ShareNet at Curricu- 
lum, Science 4-6). 
A final entry from First Officer Lisa Waters: 
“The Ecoes warn that the sludge generated from the treatment 
process is hazardous and must itself be treated in order to 
complete the treatment cycle. 
The first step in sludge treatment is to remove the water from the 
solids. This is done by gravity. Chemicals are also added to kill 
disease-causing organisms. The sludge will eventually be ‘sta- 
bilized’ (no more dangerous pollutants will exist in it) and turn 
into harmless organic matter. This material can be placed into a 
landfill, deposited into the ground or composited to be used as a 
fertilizer or soil conditioner. 
The Ecoes also mentioned that there were smaller forms of 
wastewater treatment for Ecoes or humanlife living in rural 
areas. These treatment processes are called septic Tanks, Leach 
Fields and Sand Filters. 
Septic Tanks separate the solids from the liquid. The solids 
collect on the bottom the [sic] tank and are parochially pumped 
out to a community treatment facility or an approved disposal 
site. The practically treated wastewater is piped to a leach field. 
The wastewater pumped to the leach field and is allowed to seep 
through 
the soil. The soil acts like a filter and removes bacteria 
and nutrients 
in the wastewater. The microorganisims 
in the soil 
purify 
the wastewater. 

— A-682 — 
Sand filters are used in soon [sic] areas were [sic] water tables are 
high on the soils and do not allow the water to pass through them 
easily. Sand and gravel are mounded on top of natural soil to 
filter wastewater received from septic tanks before the wastewa- 
ter reaches the natural soil. 
This information will be crucial to us if we are not rescued soon 
and the Destiny’s personnel and their families actually have to 
establish a colony here.”

Lesson 1: 
The Case of the Wasteful Water Fountain 
StarDate 2220: Chief Engineer Sun, of the disabled Starship 
Destiny, asks our help in determining whether the crew is 
wasting precious water by using a fountain. 
GRADE LEVEL: 5 
OBJECTIVES: 
Students will predict and measure the average amount of 
clean water used to drink at a drinking fountain, and 
compare amount actually consumed with amount wasted. 
INTRODUCTION: 
After studying the water cycle (lecturette in the StarDate 
2220 Narrative “Not A Drop To Drink”), students will be 
aware that there is a limited freshwater supply. This activity 
is designed to develop student awareness of: how much 
water is used during an activity as routine as getting a drink 
from the drinking fountain; relative efficiencies of one 
water delivery system over another (e.g.: fountain vs. cup 
use); and the benefit and drawbacks of each system (waste 
of water in fountain delivery vs. environmental problems of 
paper cups and infection control issues of reusable cups). 
MATERIALS: 
Stopwatch 
500 ml beakers 
two large containers (small buckets) 
calculators 
drinking glasses with ounces marked 

— A-684 — 
PROBLEMS/CAUTIONS: 
More accurate results will be obtained if students who are 
to be observed in this project do not know what researchers 
are watching for. Have “fountain watchers” try to position 
themselves out of the way and watch from a distance. If 
other students ask what the researchers are doing, have 
them reply, “we are studying water use.” If students press 
for more information, researchers may say, “we are just 
counting. But while we are researching, we’re not sup- 
posed to talk.” Explain that experimental subjects, such as 
their fellow students, tend to behave differently (in this 
case, drink longer or shorter times) if they know which of 
their actions are being observed. 
The potential of the 
behavior of the researchers to influence the actions of 
research subjects is an important piece of information about 
scientific technique. 
PROCEDURE: 
1. 
Explain to the students the procedure for collecting 
water samples. 
a) 
Using two separate containers, the students will 
collect water from their designated drinking foun- 
tains by pressing the button all the way down, 
allowing maximum water flow. One sample will 
be for 5 seconds and another for 30 seconds. 
b) 
Assign teams of two students to designated water 
fountains, one student to depress the button and 
manage the capture container and the other to 
keep time. 
2. 
After the water collection is made, the students return 
to the room and determine, in milliliters, the amount of 
water that was collected. After recording their find- 
ings the students then find the average amount of 
— A-685 — 
water that is released from the drinking fountain. 
(Other teams could be sent out to double check the first 
teams findings, or those students not collecting the 
water could re-check the volume of the amount col- 
lected.) 
Ask students to predict the amount of water used per 
student, per drinking fountain visit. 
Send the teams out into the school and have them 
“stakeout” the drinking fountain. One student will 
record the length of drink with the stopwatch, while 
the other records the number of students getting a 
drink at that particular fountain. (For example, Stu- 
dent #1...7 sec., #2...4 sec.) This should be scheduled 
at a time when students are not coming in from recess, 
so as to not inflate the results. Scheduling could be 
arranged at different times of the day. (For example, 
team #1 9:00-9:30, 
team #2 9:30-10:00, team #3 1:30- 
2:: etc.) 
With your results, find the average length of drink (this 
could be done per individual fountain), and then 
multiply that number times the number of students 
drinking during the time measured (1/2 hour). Multi- 
ply this times the number of 1/2 hour segments 
of a 
school day to calculate 
the average amount of water 
used in at a drinking fountain. 
Waste measurement: Student researchers measure the 
amount actually drunk by ten different students drink- 
ing from glasses instead of the fountain. (Note: These 
students are offered their water in a glass, on which 
ounces have been marked, when they arrive at the 
fountain. They are told to drink only what they want. 
The duration of their drinking is measured in seconds 
and amount drunk per student is then calculated and 

— A-686 — 
averaged among all glass drinkers.) These amounts 
are then averaged into a standard amount consumed. 
Researchers then collect the water expended by each 
fountain in the school, running fully open, during ten 
second intervals. 
This amount is averaged into a 
standard volume per second. The difference between 
the two is freshwater wasted. 
EVALUATION: 
Ask the students: What is the average amount of water 
used? How does their prediction compare? What are some 
possible explanations for the difference between their pre- 
diction and the actual results? Is freshwater for drinking 
efficiently conserved by the fountain delivery system? 
Why or why not? 
Explore the alternatives. Students should consider the cost 
and distribution/waste management aspects of disposable 
cups. They should research the infection control issues 
brought up by the use of re-usable cups, their expense, 
energy use to heat water for washing and time involved. 
Compare and contrast. Which choice is most environmen- 
tally sound? What are their benefits and drawbacks? 
EXTENSIONS: 
Using the volume average of fountain output already estab- 
lished in the main research protocol, students measure the 
number of drinkers at all fountains in a standard hour and 
calculate the total consumption for the whole school in one 
day? What would be total volume? For the town? What are 
other necessary activities that require clean freshwater? 
Are there any that could use recycled water? 
— A-687 —

PROCEDURE: 
l. 
Cut the plastic bottle in half. Using a rubber band 
place the nylon screen material over the mouth of the 
bottle and secure around the bottle’s neck with a 
rubber band. 
Push the cotton ball into the neck of the bottle from the 
inside and invert the bottle so the mouth is pointing 
down. Place it into the other half of the bottle so the 
bottom half acts as a catcher for the liquid as it runs 
through 
the filter. With the ground up charcoal and 
sand, alternate layers of approximately 1 - 1.5 cm in 
thickness over the cotton ball starting with the sand, 
then the charcoal, then more sand, etc. 
In a 500 mi beaker, add the 100 mi of soil to 400 mi of 
water and stir. Add a few drops of food coloring and 
the vinegar. Mix thoroughly 
and find the mass of the 
mixture on the balance and record. 
After finding the mass, make sure the contents are

When all of the muddy mix has been emptied out,

— A-688 — 
5. 
Let contents filter overnight. 
6.

find the mass of the filtered mixture. Record mass and 
observations. 
EVALUATION: 
The following questions will help students make their 
observations: 
1. 
What physical changes have taken place in the filtra- 
tion? Consider clarity and color of the water. 
2. 
Has the smell of the water changed? What in the filter 
could have been responsible for this change? 
3. 
If filtered again, what will happen to the water? 
EXTENSIONS:

water purification a different “medium’’) such as small 
pebbles, crushed limestone, or different types of soils. 
Try testing other water traits like pH and other liquids 
mixed in for smell or clarity. What conclusions can be 
drawn about how to reduce water pollution? 
2. 
Water that is not potable (safely drinkable) is often 
also dangerous to many plant and animal species for 
whom it is home. What are some life forms that may 
be destroyed by contaminated water?

Olin Environmental Project

Lession 3 
Contamination: It Can Be One In A Million 
StarDate 2220: First Officer Waters reporting: 
The Starship Destiny Away Team has been working with 
Medical Officer Aleeta Crystal to reduce crew member 
sickness from drinking contaminated water. To be certain the 
crew is fully aware of the need to avoid drinking water that 
has not been analyzed in the Destiny’s on-board laboratories, 
1 am asking each of you to perform the following experiment. 
GRADE LEVEL: 5 
OBJECTIVES: 
The student will define parts per million and parts per 
billion. 
INTRODUCTION: 
Parts per million or parts per billion is a measurement of the 
dilution of a chemical that is in minute concentrations. This 
analytical process can be used to test such things as ground 
water for contamination. As pollutants are absorbed into 
the soil, they are greatly diluted with rainwater. Even toxins 
in great dilution, in which only very small amounts of the 
toxin remain in a great amount of clean water, can still be 
dangerous 
to living things. Its detection 
and measurement 
can be determined and quantified in the small parts we call 
parts 
per million and parts per billion. 
MATERIALS: 
* — Red or Blue food coloring 
* 
Water 
* 
5 clear containers (glass beakers or jars) 
* 
Dropper 

Bis 
— A-690 — 
PREPARATION: 
Tell the students that even though they cannot see any 
evidence of pollution in water, this does not mean that it is 
not present. Pollutants in our ground water are measured in 
parts per million and parts per billion. Even very small 
amounts can be hazardous. Some examples of what these 
numbers mean are: 
“One Part Per Million” (1 ppm) - Is equal to: one step 
on a walk (north to south) across Minnesota; one 
second 
in 11.7 days; 
a 
pitcher of tea in an olympic size 
swimming pool; a bucket of sand (1.5 Liters) in a 
football field 1 foot deep. 
“One Part Per Billion” (1 ppb) - Is equal to: one step 
on a journey nineteen times around the earth (at the 
equator); one second in 32 years; a marble in a football 
field 1 foot deep; one drop in a full railroad tank car or 
one drop in a full olympic size swimming pool. 
Remember: 
There are one thousand times one thousand in one 
million 1,000 x 1,000 = 1,000,000 
There are one thousand times one million in one 
billion 1,000 x 1,000,000 = 1,000,000,000 
PROCEDURE: 
1. 
With the dropper, put 9 drops of water into a clear 
container and label it “A”. Add one drop of food 
coloring to it and stir. This represents a dilution of 1/ 
10. Rinse the dropper after each use with water 
so as 
to not contaminate the experiment by exceeding the 
correct amount of dilution. 
2. 
With the dropper, put 9 drops of water in another 
container. Add one drop of the mixture from “A” to it 
— A-691 — 
and mix. Label this one “B”. The food coloring in 
container “B” is now diluted to 1/100. 
3. 
Continue this procedure with the other containers, 
labeling them appropriately and placing 9 drops of 
water 
in each, followed by one drop “in the previous 
container. 
4. 
Container “C” will represent 1/1,000 dilution. “D” is 
1/100,000. “E” is finally the representation 1/1,000,000 
or one part per million. 
5. 
Question students: At what point can you no loner see 
any trace of the color? If the color had been a deadly 
toxin, at what point would you no longer be able to 
protect yourself by visually judging the water purity? 
6. 
To be certain that students don’t leave this lesson 
afraid to drink from the kitchen faucet, assure them 
that their water is tested every day with very sophisti- 
cated equipment and chemical detectors that routinely 
analyze at the level of ppm and ppb. 
EVALUATION: 
What does part per million mean? (One drop of pollutant 
to one million drops of water.) 
What does part per billion mean? (One drop of pollutant to 
one billion drops of water - the full olympic pool.) 
EXTENSIONS: 
This same demonstration can be done with gasoline instead 
of food coloring to show that even our smell cannot detect 
the presence 
of a chemical. 
The human threshold 
for 
gasoline is about five parts per billion. What kinds of water 
tests are done to determine the quality of your home water 
supply? Determine other examples of parts per million. 

— A-692 — 
— A-693 —
