The Olin Project for Environmental Science 
Spring Water Quality Project, 1993 
Update from the Olin Environmental Project’s Teacher Partici- 
pants 
Written by Wallie Winholtz, Lead Teacher 
Spring Project 
Our initial project includes the following water quality tests: 
Dissolved Oxygen, pH and temperature measurement, and study 
of the benthic macroinvertebrates. 
So as to not reinvent the 
wheel, many of the procedures we followed for data collection 
came from the Field Guide for Water Quality Monitoring. An 
Environmental Education Programs for Schools by Mark K. 
Mitchell and William 
B. Stapp. Their procedures have been tried 
and proven to be reliable 
and have been the basis for many water 
projects across the country. 
Before going out to conduct our tests, the teachers involved 
became familiar with the testing on a weekend outing to a local 
Stream. To prepare 
the students for the data collection 
proce- 

— A-696 — 
dures, each separate test was discussed and practiced in the 
classroom using tapwater collected from students’ homes. This 
made our use of time in the field more efficient. 
Data Collection Procedures - Dissolved Oxygen 
The following is a description of some general information about 
collecting a sample of water for testing dissolved oxygen. 
Because dissolved oxygen (DO) levels vary so much according 
to time, weather, and temperature, this test should be run during 
the same period (week and time of day), if yearly comparisons 
are to made [sic]. 
It is best to sample away from shore and below the water surface. 
In a free-flowing river or stream with good mixing, samples 
taken beneath the surface in the current should be a representa- 
tive sample. In slow moving water, it is best to sample away from 
shore and at various depths. Remember to test for DO immedi- 
ately after securing the sample. For specific testing procedures, 
follow the instructions that come with your particular test kit. 
The lowest DO amount (mg/L) at which fish survive for 24 hrs. 
(summer) are Black Bass 6.0, Yellow Perch 4.2, and Black 
Bullhead 3.3 Examples of macroinvertebrates that cannot toler- 
ate low levels of DO are mayfly nymphs, caddisfly larvae, and 
beetle larvae. Examples of pollution-tolerant organisms are 
worms and fly larvae. You might also find nuisance algae and 
anaerobic organisms surviving at low DO levels. 
pH 
The following are some important things to remember about 
testing for pH. 
A water sample that is less than 7 is said to be “acidic” and a 
reading over 7 is “basic”. It is important to realize that for every 
one unit change on the pH scale, there is approximately a ten-fold 
change in how acidic or basic the sample is. For example, a 
— A-697 — 
reading of 4.3 is approximately ten times more acidic than a 
reading of 5.0-5.6 and a pH of 4 is about 100 times more acidic 
than a pH of 6. If the stream contains a lot of limestone, it may 
neutralize the effects of acids. 
An extremely high or low pH makes the water unsuitable for 
. Most organisms. In lakes, a pH below 5 is a serious problem. 
Very acidic waters can cause heavymetals, such as copper and 
aluminum, to be released into the water. Heavy metals can 
accumulate 
on the gills of fish 
or cause deformities 
in young fish. 
Like DO, the sample collected should be done away from shore 
and below the surface. The sample should be measured imme- 
diately, because the temperature 
can affect the pH value. If many 
samples are tested, the most frequently reported pH value should 
be used rather than the average value. Please follow the testing 
instructions found with your test kit.

— A-698 — 
Temperature 
The temperature of a river or stream is very important for water 
quality. It can directly influence: 
1. 
the amount of oxygen dissolved in the water 
2. 
the rate of photosynthesis by algae and other aquatic 
plants 
3. 
the metabolic rates of aquatic organisms 
4. 
the sensitivity of organisms to toxic wastes, parasites, 
and diseases. 
Remember, cool water can hold more oxygen than warm water, 
because gases are more easily dissolved in cool water. Change 
in temperature can be caused by industries introducing warmer 
water to the stream, storm water runoff of warm urban surfaces, 
lack of shade on the stream due to cutting down trees, and 
suspended solids caused by soil erosion. 
As the temperature rises, the rate of photosynthesis and plant 
growth also increases. More plants grow and more plants die. 
Decomposition by bacteria consumes more oxygen. The meta- 
bolic rate of the organisms rises with an increased temperature, 
thus a higher demand for oxygen. 
Life cycles of aquatic 
organisms speed up and can cause problems for birds that feed 
on certain organisms emerging at specific periods during their 
migratory fligiiis. 
When testing water for temperature, lower the thermometer four 
inches into the water for approximately 2 minutes to obtain a 
constant reading. Record your measurement. 
If looking for 
“thermal” pollution, also take a reading approximately one mile 
up stream. Find the difference in the temperatures to find the 
temperature change. Record. 
— A-699 — 
Benthic Macroinvertebrates 
The variety of aquatic organisms can also contribute to the whole 
picture of the stream’s health. 
Some organisms are more 
plentiful in certain environments and sometimes a wide variety 
of organisms can indicate the waters ability to support life. This 
can be judged from the information gathered in a survey of 
organism diversity. 
Ideally, a survey of macroinvertebrates 
should be dispersed along the length of a river, so that changes 
in water quality and shifts in the aquatic community from 
headwaters to mouth can be noted. Sampling should be done in 
March/April or late October if only done once or twice a year. 
Collecting should be done in a riffle area, ic. a somewhat 
shallow area with steady current and rock and gravel bottom. 
Safety precautions should be taken to protect your skin. Rubber 
gloves and rubber boots are ideal. Washing hands after sampling 
is recommended. 
There are a variety of ways to collect these tiny organisms. In 
general, you are looking for a variety of macroinvertebrates to 
help in determining the stream’s health. These can be collected 
by turning over rocks and examining the undersides for the 
organisms or using a variety of nets to catch them as you stir up 
the substrate. Make sure the net is downstream from the area 
being disturbed and the organisms will flow into the net and be 
lifted from the water where the collection can take place. 
Examples of pollution-sensitive organisms are mayflies, 
stoneflies, some caddisflies, riffle beetles, and hellgramites. 
Examples of pollution-tolerant organisms are aquatic worms, 
leeches, and certain midge larva. 
Methods can be found for collecting these organisms and using 
other water quality testing measures in Mitchell and Stapp’s 
Field Guide for Water Quality Monitoring.

APPENDIX LL 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
Case No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al., 
Plaintiffs, 
v. 
THE STATE OF MISSOURL et al., 
Defendants. 
MOTION OF PLAINTIFFS FOR AN ORDER 
APPROVING PAYMENT BY THE STATE 
DEFENDANTS OF FUNDS TO STUDY AND 
PLAN THREE VIT HIGH SCHOOLS 
[Filed: Sept. 29, 1994] 
Plaintiffs move this Court to issue an order requiring the State 
of Missouri defendants to pay to the Metropolitan Community 
Colleges, of Kansas City, 
the sum of $225,000, 
to fund study and 
planning costs for VIT high schools located one each at the three 
community colleges in the Kansas City area as detailed in 
Attachment A, the proposal submitted to the Desegregation 
Monitoring Committee. 
SUGGESTIONS IN SUPPORT OF MOTION 
At its September 20, 1994 meeting, the DMC approved a 
proposal developed by Magistrate Judge Robert Larsen, ap- 
pointed by this Court to facilitate the development of voluntary 
interdistrict transfer plans. The proposal, 
if implemented, would 
create three VIT high schools, one each at Maplewoods, Penn 
Valley, and Longview Community Colleges. Each such high 
school would initially enroll 100 students as ninth graders, 

— A-714— 
possibly as early as next September. Fifty of those students at 
each of the VIT High Schools would be minority students from 
the KCMSD and fifty would be nonminority students from 
suburban school districts. Eventually, at the beginning of the 
fourth year of operation when the initial ninth graders had 
reached twelfth grade, the schools would each enroll 400 stu- 
dents, 200 minority students from the KCMSD and 200 
nonminority students from suburban districts. 
This motion proposed funding for planning such VIT High 
Schools, planning to be coordinated by the Metropolitan Com- 
munity Colleges. To date, according to statements by Magistrate 
Judge Larsen to the DMC, this proposal has received initial 
support from the KCMSD, the three community colleges, the 
Kauffman Foundation, and from the Missouri Department of 
Elementary and Secondary Education, including active partici- 
pation by and support from Commissioner Bartman in the 
informal working group which to date has met to prepare the 
outline of this VIT High School plan. 
That working group has reached agreement on the outlines of 
the plan and needs now to conduct more formal planning. It is 
expected that this planning effort will result in a detailed pro- 
posal for consideration by this Court next Spring, in time for the 
plan to be implemented in September, 1995, if sufficient partici- 
pation by the interested parties is assured. 
Plaintiffs request this Court to approve funding for this plan- 
ning effort. If the initial cooperation of the entities involved in 
developing this idea continues and the plan comes to fruition, it 
will afford an important opportunity to advance racial integra- 
tion for hundreds of KCMSD minority students who have yet to 
benefit from this Court’s 1985 order requiring State of Missouri 
defendants to develop a meaningful interdistrict transfer pro- 
gram. 
Six hundred minority high school students attending 
school outside KCMSD schools in settings equally balanced 
— A-715— 
between minority and nonminority students, is a possibility that 
should be seriously studied and developed. 
For these reasons, plaintiffs request this Court to issue its order 
approving the funding for planning VIT High Schools. 
Respectfully submitted, 
By: /s/ Arthur A. Benson, II

Dear Dr. Eubanks: 
I am writing to request that the Desegregation Monitoring 
Committee 
take action at its September 20, 1994 meeting 
on the 
following agenda item concerning voluntary interdistrict trans- 
fers advanced by the plaintiff schoolchildren. At the request of 
Judge Robert E. Larsen, who has taken the lead in developing 
this project, we ask that the DMC consider this matter in 
executive session. To that end, I am not sending copies of this 
letter and ask that the DMC distribute copies to the parties with 
the DMC’ s request that the subject matter be kept confidential by 
the parties. 
We request that the DMC take no exception to the proposal to 
seek by motion from the District Court an order directing the 
State of Missouri to fund 100%, as is usual in VIT matters, of the 
$225,000 planning costs associated with efforts to establish VIT 
s
we e

— A-718 — 
high school programs at Penn Valley, Maple Woods and 
Longview Community Colleges for opening in September, 
1995. Each such program will commence with 100 ninth grade 
students — one-half minority students from the KCMSD and the 
other half nonminority students from participating surrounding 
Missouri school districts. Each year thereafter, as the initial 
classes advance, 100 new ninth graders will enter each program. 
The program is intended to start small in order to iron out any 
unforeseen problems and, within a few years, reach the projected 
400 students per school. 
The high schools will be located on and operated by the three 
Community Colleges with the assistance of participating school 
districts, the Missouri Department of Elementary and Secondary 
Education, and the Kauffman Foundation. Students who suc- 
cessfully graduate from these schools will have earned some 
college credits and be given by the program, assistance with 
funding further educational efforts. The schools at Longview 
and Maple Woods will be geared toward the students earning 
post secondary degrees. The school at Penn Valley will focus on 
educational preparation for work. 
Funding of $225,000 is requested for the planning and budget- 
ing of this program, commencing immediately, so that the high 
school program may open next September. An informal work- 
ing group has been meeting this summer to outline the proposal 
and has reached the point where formal and full-time 
efforts are 
needed to prepare the detailed pan for final consideration by this 
Committee and the Court early next year, in time to obtain 
approval, hire staff, order materials, and recruit students for 
enrollment next fall. The breakdown of the $225,000 is as 
follows: 
— A-719 — 
Project director 
- 
$85,000 (includes fringes) 
Secretarial support 
- 
$30,000 (includes fringes) 
Consultants 
- 
$40,000 
Travel 
- 
$20,000 
Printing 
- 
$20,000 
Writers 
- 
$20,000 
Office Expenses 
- 
$10,000 
Miscellaneous 
- 
$8,000 
Office Rental 
- 
$2,000 
For these reasons, we request that the DMC take no exception 
to this request that the State of Missouri fund $225,000 in 
planning costs for the development of the VIT high school 
program. 
Yours very truly,
